Other product name(s)	N/A
Applicant	LEO PHARMA AS
Dosage form(s)/formulation(s)	CREAM
Dosing regimen	Apply twice daily to skin of the affected areas only on the hands and wrists
Applicant-proposed indication(s)/population(s)	Topical treatment of moderate to severe chronic hand eczema (CHE) in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable.

L47: ## I. Executive Summary
L48: ## 1. Overview
L49: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L50: On July 23, 2024, the Applicant (LEO Pharma A/S) submitted NDA 219155 for ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2% for the topical treatment of moderate-to-severe chronic hand eczema (CHE) in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable. The Applicant is seeking approval for ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2% under Section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act.
L51: Delgocitinib belongs to the pharmacological class of Janus kinase inhibitors (JAKi). Delgocitinib is a new molecular entity that inhibits the activity of all four Janus kinases (JAKs): JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and tyrosine kinase 2 (TYK2). To date, the only FDA approved product for the treatment of CHE are corticosteroids. However, given the potential for serious adverse reactions with long-term use of corticosteroids, there remains an unmet medical need for safe and effective therapy for CHE.
L52: The efficacy and safety of delgocitinib cream, 2% was evaluated in two 16-week vehicle-controlled trials (DELTA 1 and DELTA 2) and a 36-week open-label extension trial (DELTA 3). These trials enrolled subjects 18 years and older with moderate to severe CHE who had had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids (TCS) are not advisable. Both trials assessed the response on the primary endpoint of proportion of subjects with an Investigator's Global Assessment (IGA) score of 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) with at least a 2-point improvement from baseline at Week 16, an endpoint considered to be clinically meaningful. In both trials, delgocitinib was statistically superior to vehicle. The Applicant has demonstrated that delgocitinib is effective for its intended use in the proposed target population and has met the evidentiary standard required by 21 CFR 314.126(a)(b) to support approval of this NDA.
L53: The safety database included a total of 638 subjects who received treatment with delgocitinib twice daily for 16 weeks and 198 subjects who received delgocitinib twice daily for 52 weeks. The most common adverse reactions that were reported in the delgocitinib group were application site pain, paresthesia, pruritus, erythema, bacterial skin infections including finger cellulitis, paronychia, other skin infections, leukopenia, and neutropenia.
L54: Treatment of inflammatory conditions with products from the JAKi class is associated with the risk of serious and life-threatening adverse reactions. Given the systemic exposure to delgocitinib after topical application, observed adverse reactions, the size of safety database, and the duration of treatment with delgocitinib during clinical trials, the potential risks related to JAK inhibition cannot be excluded. However, the review team concluded that these uncertainties do not preclude approval, as long as they are appropriately described in the Warnings and Precautions section of the product labeling. Approval of this product will also include a postmarketing requirement to further characterize the long-term safety with data from a
L55: NDA 219155
ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L56: randomized, active-controlled clinical trial in subjects with moderate to severe CHE. This trial should be of sufficient size and duration to evaluate safety events of interest.
L57: Furthermore, additional postmarketing requirements will focus on the collection of safety data in populations that have not yet been studied, such as lactating women and pediatric patients. There are no data in breastfeeding women receiving delgocitinib on the presence of delgocitinib in human milk. Postmarketing requirements will include a study in lactating women who have received treatment with delgocitinib to assess concentrations of delgocitinib in breastmilk.
L58: Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (21 U.S.C. 355c), all applications for new active ingredients (which includes new salts and new fixed combinations), new indications, new dosage forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are required to contain an assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the claimed indication in pediatric patients unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or inapplicable. We are waiving the pediatric study requirement for ages 0 to less than 12 years of age because necessary studies are impossible or highly impracticable. We are deferring submission of pediatric studies for ages 12 to 17 years of age for this application because this product is ready for approval for use in adults and the pediatric study has not been completed.
L59: The overall benefit-risk of delgocitinib cream, 2% is favorable for the proposed indication as described in Section 2.1 Benefit-Risk Framework below. The review team recommends approval of this new drug application with the above-described labeling and postmarketing requirements. The signatories agree with the assessment and recommendations from the team.
L60: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L61: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and well-controlled clinical investigations.
L62: The Applicant provided substantial evidence of effectiveness with data from two adequate and well-controlled trials, DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, that evaluated delgocitinib cream, 2% twice daily (BID) compared with vehicle for 16 weeks. The trials enrolled subjects ages 18 years and older with a clinical diagnosis of CHE with disease severity graded as moderate to severe according to Investigator's Global Assessment (IGA-CHE) (i.e., an IGA-CHE score of 3 or 4) who have a documented recent history of inadequate response to treatment with TCS (within 1 year prior to the screening visit) or for whom TCS are documented to be otherwise medically inadvisable (e.g., due to important side effects or safety risks).
L63: The IGA-CHE scale was used to define the study population and conduct the primary efficacy evaluation at the prespecified timepoint. The IGA-CHE scale is a single-item, clinician-reported outcome (ClinRO) designed to evaluate overall CHE severity using a 5-level response scale ranging from 0 (clear) to 4 (severe). The IGA-CHE was determined to be fit-for-purpose for assessing CHE severity to support regulatory decision-making. The primary efficacy endpoint is IGA-CHE treatment success (TS), which is defined as a score of 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) with at least a 2-point improvement from baseline at Week 16. Delgocitinib cream, 2% was
L64: NDA 219155
ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L65: superior to vehicle (\le 0.025) in both trials on the primary efficacy endpoint. The results on the primary efficacy endpoint were:
L66: - DELTA 1- placebo: 9.9% versus delgocitinib, 2%: 19.7%
- DELTA 2- placebo: 6.9% versus delgocitinib, 2%: 29.1%
L67: Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 included many secondary endpoints adjusted for multiplicity, including IGA-CHE response at multiple timepoints, Hand Eczema Severity Index (HECSI) scores, and patient-reported outcome responses based on the Hand Eczema Symptom Diary (HESD), Dermatology Life Quality Index (DLQI) score, and Hand Eczema Impact Scale (HEIS) score.
L68: The secondary endpoints included the subject assessment of the key symptoms of CHE, itch and pain, using HESD subscores. The HESD Itch score is a weekly average of daily itch severity on an 11-point scale from 0 to 10 that assesses the maximal intensity of pruritus in the last 24 hours, with 0 being no pruritus and 10 being the worst pruritus. The HESD Pain score is a weekly average of daily pain severity on an 11-point scale from 0 to 10 that assesses the maximal intensity of pain in the last 24 hours, with 0 being no pain and 10 being the worst pain. For both Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, delgocitinib was statistically superior to vehicle on the percent of responders with HESD Itch \ge 4-point improvement and HESD Pain \ge 4-point improvement. The HESD Itch and HESD Pain scores were determined to be fit-for-purpose for assessing CHE-associated itch and pain symptoms, respectively, to support regulatory decision-making.
L69: The results of the primary and above secondary endpoints will be included in product labeling (Section 14).
L70: (b) (4)
L71: The review team concluded, and the signatories agree, that the Applicant has provided substantial evidence of effectiveness to support the indication of the treatment of moderate-to-severe chronic hand eczema. For additional discussion of the efficacy results, refer to Section 6.
L72: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L73: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L74: The Applicant submitted an NDA for delgocitinib cream, 2%, for the proposed indication of moderate-to-severe CHE in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable.
L75: Delgocitinib is a JAK inhibitor, which inhibits the activity of JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2. Janus kinases are intracellular enzymes that transmit signals arising from cytokine or growth factor-receptor interactions on the cellular membrane to influence cellular processes of hematopoiesis and immune cell function.
L76: Chronic hand eczema is a persistent inflammatory skin condition affecting the hands and wrists with a high prevalence of 9 to 10% annually and 15% lifetime in the general population. The
L77: condition involves skin barrier dysfunction and multiple inflammatory pathways (Th1, Th2, Th17, and JAK pathways), with etiology predominantly related to allergic contact dermatitis, irritant contact dermatitis, or atopic hand dermatitis. CHE significantly impairs quality of life, causing work-related disabilities, psychological burden including anxiety and depression, and social isolation. Current CHE management begins with nonpharmacologic approaches including allergen/irritant identification and avoidance, emollients, and behavioral modifications. Topical corticosteroids, indicated for the relief of the inflammatory and pruritic manifestations of corticosteroid-responsive dermatoses, have been the standard of care for CHE treatment for all age groups (Eichenfield et al. 2014). Other topical agents approved for atopic dermatitis (topical calcineurin inhibitors, phosphodiesterase-4 inhibitors) are used off-label for CHE. For moderate-to-severe CHE with inadequate topical response, systemic therapies include dupilumab (used off-label) and immunosuppressive agents (cyclosporin, methotrexate, mycophenolate mofetil, azathioprine), but these carry significant safety concerns, including potential for rebound, hypersensitivity reactions, and systemic toxicities, that limit long-term use. Therefore, there is an unmet need for treatment options.
L78: To establish the safety and effectiveness of delgocitinib cream, 2% for the treatment of moderate-to-severe CHE, the Applicant submitted data from two adequate and well-controlled clinical trials of similar design. Trials 1401 (DELTA 1, enrolled 487 adult subjects) and 1402 (DELTA 2, enrolled 472 adult subjects) were randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials that evaluated delgocitinib cream, 2% applied BID compared to vehicle for 16 weeks. Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 were followed by an open-label extension period up to Week 52 in Trial DELTA 3.
L79: Enrolled subjects were 18 years of age and older with moderate-to-severe CHE (defined as an IGA-CHE score of 3 or 4) with a history of inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable. The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of subjects who achieved IGA-CHE TS at Week 16, defined as a score of 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) with at least a 2-point improvement from baseline. In Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, delgocitinib was statistically superior to vehicle (19.7% versus 9.9% and 29.1% versus 6.9%, respectively).
L80: Other key endpoints that supported the efficacy of the delgocitinib cream, 2% included the proportion of subjects with:
L81: - HESD itch \ge4-point improvement from Baseline to Week 16 (47% active versus 23% vehicle for Trial DELTA 1; 47% active versus 20% vehicle for Trial DELTA 2),
- HESD Pain \ge4-point improvement from Baseline to Week 16 (49% active versus 28% vehicle for Trial DELTA 1; 49% active versus 23% vehicle for Trial DELTA 2).
L82: The primary safety database consisted of pooled data from subjects from the 16-week vehicle-controlled period of Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, and up to 36 additional weeks of open-label treatment in Trial DELTA 3. The primary safety database included 638 subjects exposed to delgocitinib BID. For subjects who rolled over into open-label Trial DELTA 3, a total of 198 subjects were continuously exposed to delgocitinib BID for 52 weeks.
L83: Higher rates of the following treatment-emergent adverse events were reported in subjects who received delgocitinib in the pooled Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 compared to vehicle: COVID-19 (11.1% versus 10.6%), upper respiratory tract infection (1.4% versus 0.3%), hypertension (1.1% versus 0%), back pain (1.1% versus 0.3%), and influenza (1.1% versus
L84: ## NDA 219155
## ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L85: 0.6%). Review by the clinical team of narrative summaries, timing of onset, vital signs and ECGs, subject comorbidities, and evaluation of potential alternative causes did not support a causal relationship between delgocitinib and these adverse events. Accordingly, none of these events are considered adverse reactions, and the review team determined no adverse reactions occurred in \ge 1\% subjects on delgocitinib cream and greater than vehicle. Adverse reactions that occurred in &lt;1\% of subjects who received delgocitinib and proposed for labeling include eczema herpeticum, herpes zoster, basal cell carcinoma, application site pain, paresthesia, pruritus, erythema, bacterial skin infections including finger cellulitis, paronychia, other skin infections, leukopenia, and neutropenia.
L86: Analysis of hematologic parameters showed a numerical imbalance in worsening grade shifts for leukocytes in subjects who received delgocitinib (13.6%, Exposure-Adjusted Incidence Rate (EAIR) 44.2) compared to subjects who received vehicle (6.2%, EAIR 21.3) during the 16-week vehicle-controlled period. Smaller imbalances were observed for neutrophils (4.4%, EAIR 14.2 for delgocitinib versus 3.7%, EAIR 12.8 for vehicle) and lymphocytes (2.5%, EAIR 8.1 for delgocitinib versus 1.9%, EAIR 6.4 for vehicle). The leukocyte imbalance was primarily driven by shifts from Grade 0 at baseline to Grade 1 postbaseline. There were select cases where a causal relationship with delgocitinib to leukopenia and neutropenia treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) could not be ruled out and cases in which subjects never returned to their pretreatment hematologic values. Products from the class of JAKi are associated with cytopenia in patients treated for inflammatory conditions. Therefore, the review team recommends including leukopenia and neutropenia as adverse reactions occurring in &lt;1\% of subjects in Section 6 of labeling.
L87: Labeling for JAK inhibitor products approved for the treatment of inflammatory conditions carry Boxed Warnings for serious and life-threatening adverse reactions including: serious infections (tuberculosis, bacterial, invasive fungal, viral, and opportunistic infections) leading to hospitalization or death; malignancies including lymphoma and nonmelanoma skin cancers; increased all-cause mortality including sudden cardiovascular death; major cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, stroke); and thrombosis (deep venous thrombosis, pulmonary embolism, arterial thrombosis).
L88: The review team considered the following factors to inform the recommendation for delgocitinib labeling that will adequately address the adverse reactions observed during the clinical trials as well as the uncertainties of the rare and latent safety risks associated with products of the JAK inhibitor class.
L89: - Measurable systemic exposure: in the maximal use trial in subjects with CHE (body surface area [BSA] range 0.25 to 3%), delgocitinib demonstrated systemic absorption (range 0.01 to 7.30 ng/mL [0.03 to 23.52nM]) with median maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) of 0.61 ng/mL (1.96nM). One subject reached a concentration of 29.2 ng/mL (94.09nM). This does not represent negligible systemic exposure defined as subnanomolar.
- Absence of established safety threshold: no scientifically established systemic exposure threshold exists below which JAK inhibitor-related adverse events do not occur. Therefore, the potential for adverse reactions observed with products from JAKi class cannot be excluded.
- Adverse events observed during clinical trials: neutropenia and leukopenia were observed during the controlled period of the clinical trials. Also, during the clinical trials, cases of herpes zoster and a case of eczema herpeticum were reported. Given the mechanism of action
L90: of JAKi, a role for delgocitinib cannot be excluded. In addition, postmarketing data for the product approved in Japan (not approved in the United States) with the same active moiety (delgocitinib ointment, 0.5%) revealed 86 cases of eczema herpeticum and 21 cases of herpes zoster, including in pediatric patients. Although the causality cannot be established from postmarketing reports, a role for delgocitinib cannot be excluded.
L91: - Extent of safety database: treatment of inflammatory conditions with products from JAKi class is associated with serious and life-threatening adverse reactions. Given the size of safety database and duration of treatment with delgocitinib during clinical trials, potential risks related to JAK inhibition cannot be excluded.
- Delgocitinib will be used on a limited BSA
L92: The proposed indication is for the treatment of patients with moderate-to-severe CHE in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable. Labeling for delgocitinib cream should include a Warning and Precaution section describing potential risks related to JAK inhibition. A Boxed Warning is not warranted at this time because delgocitinib labeling will specify application to the hands and wrists only (&lt;5% BSA) and include a restriction on the amount of product used in 2 weeks and 1 month which may mitigate the risk of off-label use on a larger body surface area.
L93: Given the residual uncertainties about JAK-inhibitor-related risks for adverse reaction (AR), the FDA will require a postmarketing study (PMR) to evaluate the long-term safety to further characterize the risk profile and ensure appropriate risk mitigation for this drug product. A PMR will be issued to the Applicant to conduct a randomized, active-controlled trial of sufficient size and duration to characterize safety events of interest including cytopenia and viral reactivation.
L94: Furthermore, additional postmarketing requirements will focus on the collection of safety data in populations that have not yet been studied such as lactating women and pediatric patients.
L95: In conclusion, based on the available safety and efficacy data, the benefit-risk of delgocitinib cream, 2% used twice daily is favorable in the population of adult patients with moderate to severe chronic hand eczema who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable. The review team recommends approval of NDA 219155 with the agreed upon labeling and PMRs, as detailed above. The signatories agree with these recommendations. This approval will address an unmet need for patients who suffer from moderate to severe chronic hand eczema who have had an inadequate response to or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable.
L96: **Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework**
L97: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Chronic hand eczema (CHE) is a heterogeneous, fluctuating, pruritic and painful inflammatory skin disease of the hands and wrists that persists for more than 3 months or recurs at least twice per year (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Borg et al. 2024; Sheehan 2025). The clinical manifestations may include dry, scaly, erythematous and pruritic hand macules or papules that may be accompanied by vesicles, pustules, edema, linear fissures or erosions, and hyperkeratosis (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Tancredi et al. 2023; Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023). Lesions are usually bilateral and may involve the palmar or dorsal surface or both. In long-standing disease, nail changes may be seen (Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023; Sheehan 2025). CHE has an estimated 1-year prevalence of 9 to 10% and a lifetime prevalence of 15% in the general population, with a similar prevalence among adolescents and adults (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Tancredi et al. 2023; Borg et al. 2024; Sheehan 2025). Risk factors for CHE include concurrent atopic dermatitis (especially in children), frequent hand washing or exposure to wet conditions, and exposures to irritants or allergens in occupational (especially wet work) or domestic settings (including various substances found in cosmetics, jewelry, clothing, and medications) (Tancredi et al. 2023; Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023; Borg et al. 2024). High-risk occupations include health care workers, food handlers, hairdressers, and cleaners (Cheng et al. 2022; Sheehan 2025).
An etiology-based approach categorizes CHE as predominately related to allergic contact dermatitis (ACD), irritant contact hand dermatitis (ICD), or atopic hand dermatitis (Silverberg et al. 2021; Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Tancredi et al. 2023; Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023) though these categories may overlap. (Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023) ACD is a hypersensitivity reaction to a chemical substance such as nickel, chromate, rubber, or preservatives (Ho and Molin 2023). ICD arises from prolonged exposure to irritants, including detergents, solvents, sweat, and water, that compromise the skin barrier (Ho and Molin 2023), while atopic hand eczema is the most common
CHE represents a condition with significant unmet medical need, particularly for patients with moderate to severe disease who have inadequate response to or cannot use topical corticosteroids. The chronic nature of the condition and its substantial impact on patients' quality of life, work capacity, and psychological well-being support the need for effective treatment options beyond current therapies.
L98: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
form of CHE (Ho and Molin 2023). The pathogenesis of CHE is multifactorial with a complex interplay of genetics, environment, and exposures (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023). Skin barrier dysfunction results in altered moisture retention, with reduction in ceramides in the stratum corneum, downregulation of structural barrier proteins such as filaggrin (i.e., FLG, FLG-2) and hornerin (HRNR), and decreased expression of enzymes involved in lipid metabolism (Ho and Molin 2023; Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023) leading to increased susceptibility to irritant and contact dermatitis. Hyperkeratosis occurs when contact allergens and/or irritants trigger the release of keratinocyte-derived cytokines such as tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-alpha that promote inflammation and epidermal hyperproliferation, resulting in thickened skin and scale formation (Ho and Molin 2023). CHE involves several cytokine pathways, including Th1, Th2 and Th17, that vary according to the main triggers (Ho and Molin 2023). Exposure to irritants (cytotoxic or chemical) that cause direct skin damage results in innate immune system responses with a Th1/Th17 immune profile in ICD (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Tancredi et al. 2023). In contrast, ACD is characterized by a delayed type IV adaptive immune response induced by contact with allergens such as nickel or fragrance, leading to the selection of both CD4+ and CD8+ T lymphocytes, with specific Th1 differentiation (Tancredi et al. 2023). Meanwhile, atopic hand eczema has a predominant Th2-type response with increased expression of type 2 immune cytokines (e.g., IL-4 and IL-13) (Tancredi et al. 2023; Zalewski and Szepietowski 2023). In patients with an atopic predisposition, there is also activation of Janus kinase (JAK) pathways (Ho and Molin 2023).
CHE significantly impacts quality of life, with negative effects that range from work-related disabilities, productivity loss, impaired work functioning, missed school, and impaired sleep. CHE can contribute to a considerable psychological burden with poor self-esteem, anxiety and/or depression, and social isolation (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Tancredi et al. 2023; Borg et al. 2024).
L99: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	The initial approach to CHE management is nonpharmacologic, including confirmation of the diagnosis by exclusion of dermatologic mimickers such as tinea and scabies, identification of relevant allergens and irritants, and the regular use of emollients for hand moisturization and skin hydration (Ho and Molin 2023). In addition, behavioral modification is important when irritant or allergic triggers are present, including avoidance of allergens, irritants, and exacerbating activities and exposures (such as excessive hand washing) and use of personal protective equipment, such as vinyl or other nonlatex gloves and barrier creams (Sheehan 2025).
Topical corticosteroids (TCSs), indicated for the relief of the inflammatory and pruritic manifestations of corticosteroid-responsive dermatoses, have been the mainstay of initial CHE treatment for all age groups (Eichenfield et al. 2014) and are usually part of the initial treatment plan. However, the long-term use of TCS is associated with adverse events such as epidermal atrophy with further epidermal barrier dysfunction. Healthcare providers frequently use other topical products approved for the treatment of atopic dermatitis (AD) as steroid-sparing agents in CHE treatment, including topical calcineurin inhibitors (TCIs) such as tacrolimus or pimecrolimus, with anti-inflammatory effects, and phosphodiesterase-4 (PDE4) inhibitors such as crisaborole and roflumilast, which regulate the production of inflammatory cytokines through the degradation of cyclic adenosine monophosphate (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023).
Management of patients with moderate to severe CHE with inadequate response to topical therapies includes the use of systemic products indicated for atopic dermatitis (though none are specifically approved for this indication in the United States). Alitretinoin is approved only in Europe and a few other countries worldwide, indicated for treatment severe CHE unresponsive to treatment with potent TCS; it is not approved in the United States for this indication. Although the use of systemic corticosteroids may provide rapid improvement, discontinuation of the drug may result in recurrence of the disease with greater severity (rebound). Due to the potential for adverse effects and
While topical corticosteroids are FDA-approved for the relief of inflammatory and pruritic manifestations of corticosteroid-responsive dermatoses and have been the standard of care for treatment of CHE, long-term TCS use is associated with adverse events such as HPA axis suppression, epidermal atrophy and further barrier dysfunction.
Other topical agents approved for atopic dermatitis (topical calcineurin inhibitors, PDE4 inhibitors) are used off-label for CHE. For moderate to severe CHE with inadequate topical response, systemic therapies include dupilumab (used off-label) and immunosuppressive agents (cyclosporin, methotrexate, mycophenolate mofetil, azathioprine), but these carry significant safety concerns, including potential for rebound, hypersensitivity reactions, and systemic toxicities, that limit long-term use.
Systemic corticosteroids may provide rapid improvement, but discontinuation may result in disease recurrence with greater severity.
While topical corticosteroids are approved and effective for many patients, there remains an unmet need for patients with moderate to severe CHE who have inadequate response to TCS or for whom TCS are not advisable. Available systemic alternatives are used off-label and carry safety risks that may limit their long-term use, creating a treatment gap for this patient population.
Approval of delgocitinib would provide another option for the topical treatment of moderate to severe CHE in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable.
L100: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
rebound, the American Academy of Dermatology recommends that systemic corticosteroids generally be avoided in the treatment of AD because potential risks generally outweigh the benefits (Sidbury et al. 2014). Potential adverse effects include reversible hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis suppression with the potential for glucocorticoid insufficiency, hyperglycemia, and other endocrine effects. A particular concern in children and adolescents is the risk of decreased linear growth during treatment (Sidbury et al. 2014). Other systemic agents such as dupilumab, an IL-4 receptor and IL-13 receptor-alpha receptor antagonist, is used off-label. Current labeling includes warnings for potential adverse reactions including hypersensitivity, conjunctivitis, and keratitis and an association with eosinophilic disease in subjects treated for asthma. Other immunosuppressive agents such as cyclosporin, methotrexate, mycophenolate mofetil, and azathioprine can provide temporary remission in some patients, but their adverse reaction profiles may limit their long-term use (Cheng et al. 2022; Ho and Molin 2023; Tancredi et al. 2023).
Second-line, nonpharmacologic therapies include ultraviolet light (UV) phototherapy, specifically local psoralen plus UVA therapy (PUVA), which has been reported to improve investigator-rated control of hand eczema compared to narrow-band UVB therapy. However, the need for frequent clinic visits that are required to achieve treatment success makes this treatment a challenge for patients (Ho and Molin 2023).

Benefit	Efficacy was evaluated using the Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema (IGA-CHE) score, which is the investigator's overall assessment of chronic hand eczema severity at a given time point on a scale ranging from 0 (clear) to 4 (severe). Delgocitinib 20 mg/g was superior to in both trials for the primary endpoint of IGA-CHE Treatment Success (defined as a score of 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) with at least a 2-point improvement from baseline).DELTA 1 IGA response: 20% active versus 10% vehicle (difference from vehicle [95% CI] 10% [4%, 16%])
DELTA 2 IGA response: 29% active versus 7% vehicle (difference from vehicle [95% CI] 22% [16%, 29%])

The submitted data has met the evidentiary standard for providing substantial evidence of effectiveness in subjects with moderate to severe CHE in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable.
L101: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	Other key endpoints that supported the efficacy of the 20 mg/g BID dosage of delgocitinib included the proportion of subjects with HESD itch \ge4-point improvement from baseline to Week 16 (47% active and 23% vehicle for Trial DELTA 1; 47% active and 20% vehicle for Trial DELTA 2) and HESD pain \ge4-point improvement from baseline to Week 16 (49% active and 28% vehicle for Trial DELTA 1; 49% active and 23% vehicle for Trial DELTA 2).
The safety profile of delgocitinib was characterized in 638 subjects exposed for 16 weeks in vehicle-controlled trials and 198 subjects with continuous exposure for 52 weeks.
Delgocitinib achieves measurable systemic exposure with plasma concentrations that, though low, are not considered negligible.
No adverse reactions occurred in \ge1% subjects on delgocitinib cream compared to vehicle. Adverse reactions <1% that will be communicated in labeling include: eczema herpeticum, herpes zoster, basal cell carcinoma, application site pain, paresthesia, pruritus, erythema, bacterial skin infections including finger cellulitis, paronychia, other skin infections, leukopenia, and neutropenia.
In the vehicle-controlled 16-week period, 638 subjects received delgocitinib [PYO =197] and 321 subjects received vehicle [PYO =94], reflecting both a 2:1 randomization scheme and greater exposure time in the delgocitinib group. During this period a numerical imbalance was observed in subjects experiencing any worsening grade shifts in leukocytes (13.6% delgocitinib versus 6.2% vehicle). While it is acknowledged that the unequal exposure would be expected to result in more events in the delgocitinib group, an imbalance in hematologic parameters persists even when accounting for exposure differences through rate calculations: EAIR 44.2 versus 21.3.
Cases of herpes zoster in subjects <50 years of age and a case of eczema herpeticum were reported in the open-label period. Postmarketing data from the same moiety (CORECTIM) revealed additional possible immune-related events including 86 cases of eczema herpeticum and 21 cases of herpes zoster. Although the observed events may be consistent with
While the clinical safety database supports approval, the totality of evidence demonstrates that delgocitinib achieves measurable systemic exposure (with plasma concentrations, though low, not considered negligible) and may increase the risk of systemic adverse reactions.
Given the known serious risks associated with JAK inhibitors for inflammatory conditions and the evidence of potential systemic immunosuppressive effects in the development program, prescribers and patients should be informed of potential risks.
The identified and potential risks can be adequately managed through comprehensive labeling that includes potential risks of JAK inhibition.
Given the residual uncertainties about potential risks associated with JAK inhibition due to the limited safety database, a long-term safety postmarketing requirement (PMR) is necessary to further characterize the risk profile and ensure appropriate risk mitigation for this drug product. A PMR will be issued for an active-controlled trial. The trial should be of sufficient size and duration to characterize safety events of interest (e.g., cytopenias and viral reactivation).

The safety database size and duration may not fully capture rare or delayed adverse events associated with JAK inhibition (e.g., cytopenias, viral reactivation).

L105: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L106: The benefit-risk assessment for delgocitinib cream supports approval for the treatment of moderate to severe chronic hand eczema in adults who have had an inadequate response to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable.
L107: ## **Benefits**
L108: Delgocitinib provides substantial evidence of effectiveness based on two adequate and well-controlled trials enrolling 960 subjects. The primary efficacy endpoint demonstrated statistically significant improvements with treatment differences of 10% (DELTA 1) and 22% (DELTA 2) compared to vehicle. Secondary endpoints showed clinically meaningful improvements in itch (47% versus 20 to 23% vehicle) and pain (49% versus 23 to 28% vehicle), with 4-point or greater improvements on 11-point scales. These results represent meaningful therapeutic benefit for a condition that significantly impacts quality of life, with patients experiencing work-related disabilities and psychological burden.
L109: ## **Risks**
L110: The safety of delgocitinib was evaluated in two randomized, double-blind, multicenter, vehicle-controlled clinical trials in which 959 adults with moderate to severe CHE received delgocitinib or vehicle cream topically twice daily for 16 weeks. A total of 638 subjects were treated with delgocitinib. In both trials, adverse reactions that were reported in \le 1\% of subjects in the delgocitinib group were application site pain, paresthesia, pruritus, erythema, bacterial skin infections including finger cellulitis, paronychia, other skin infections, leukopenia, and neutropenia. In an open label extension Trial 3, 801 subjects were treated for up to an additional 36 weeks after completing Trials 1 or 2. A total of 198 subjects received continuous treatment with delgocitinib for 52 weeks. Eczema herpeticum was observed in one subject and herpes zoster was observed in two subjects treated with delgocitinib.
L111: There was measurable systemic exposure to delgocitinib in maximal use trial (range 0.01 - 7.30 ng/mL [0.03 - 23.52 nM] with median C_{max} of 0.61 ng/mL (1.96nM). One subject reached a concentration of 29.2 ng/mL (94.09nM). This does not represent negligible systemic exposure defined as subnanomolar. Data from clinical trials during the first 16 weeks (controlled period) demonstrated imbalances in hematologic parameters (13.6% versus 6.2% vehicle experiencing worsening leukocyte shifts, EAIR 44.2 versus 21.3). Cytopenias are a well-recognized adverse reaction associated with treatment with products of JAKi class.
L112: In addition, adverse events, including herpes zoster in healthy subjects under 50 years of age and eczema herpeticum were observed. JAK inhibitors can impair natural killer (NK) cell function because JAK-signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) signaling is crucial for NK cell development, activation, and function. JAKi can increase the risk of viral reactivation, particularly herpes zoster due to immunosuppressive effects. As NK cells in patients with eczema herpeticum (EH) exhibit reduced activity and altered functionality. Also, because delgocitinib is systemically absorbed, the role of delgocitinib in the development of EH and herpes zoster (HZ) cannot be excluded.
L113: The Applicant provided postmarketing data from the same active moiety (delgocitinib ointment, 0.5%: CORECTIM). There were 86 cases of EH and 21 cases of HZ, including pediatric
L114: NDA 219155
ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L115: patients. Although, it is not possible to establish causality due to limited information for these postmarking reports, a role of delgocitinib cannot be excluded.
L116: The safety database for delgocitinib of fewer than 700 subjects for 16 weeks and fewer than 200 subjects for 52 weeks is not sufficiently large to exclude adverse reactions seen with JAKi products.
L117: ## **Risk Management**
L118: Physician labeling and a Medication Guide are sufficient for risk management. Under 21 CFR 208.1, the Medication Guide is required to help prevent serious adverse events. Risk evaluation and mitigation strategies is not required.
L119: ## **Overall Assessment**
L120: The Applicant has demonstrated that delgocitinib cream, 2% is safe and effective for its intended use in the proposed target population. Given the residual uncertainties about JAK inhibitor-related risks for AR, post-marketing requirements will include an evaluation of the long-term safety, and collection of safety data in populations that have not been studied, such as lactating women and pediatric patients.
L121: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L122: ## 3. Introduction
L123: Key efficacy and safety review issues are identified below.
L124: ## 3.1. Review Issue List
L125: ## 3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L126: ## 3.1.1.1. Impact of the Lack of Run-In Period on the Assessment of Efficacy
L127: At the End-of-Phase 2 meeting, the Agency advised that an inadequate response to TCS therapy may not be reliably determined based on medical history alone and recommended incorporating a run-in period with standard care measures (e.g., moisturizers, irritant avoidance) and TCS therapy to prospectively confirm inadequate response. The Applicant did not incorporate a run-in period in their phase 3 trials and expressed concern that such a run-in period would unnecessarily re-expose subjects to therapies previously deemed ineffective and delay access to an investigational product in a setting with limited treatment options.
L128: In response to Agency concerns regarding appropriate definition of the study population, the Applicant analyzed data under TCS run-in-like conditions within the phase 3 trials (comparing subjects who discontinued daily TCS no earlier than 2, 4, or 8 weeks prior to screening, and subjects who used daily TCS on the hands after screening, up to 2 weeks before baseline). See Section 6.3.1.
L129: ## 3.1.1.2. Impact of Content Validity, Other Measurement Properties, and Data Interpretability for COA-Based Efficacy Endpoints
L130: There was uncertainty regarding whether the HECI was fit-for-purpose and interpretable(b) (4). There was limited validity evidence and the scoring algorithm was not clearly interpretable. Refer to Section 16.1.3.3 for details.
L131: There was uncertainty regarding whether the HEIS proximal daily activity limitations (PDAL) was fit-for-purpose and interpretable(b) (4). Validity evidence provided for the items and score for the HEIS PDAL did not demonstrate that the content was relevant and interpretable given CHE type and physician-rated CHE severity. Refer to Section 16.1.2 for details.
L132: ## 3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
L133: ## 3.1.2.1. Impact of the Formulation Change on the Assessment of Pharmacokinetics and Safety
L134: The Applicant used the final to-be-marketed formulation, containing (b)(4) mg/g [REDACTED] (b)(4), in the majority of clinical trials, including maximal use studies. However, an earlier formulation containing (b)(4) mg/g [REDACTED] (b)(4) was also used during development. The change was adequately supported by the submitted data, and it was determined to have no impact on product performance or clinical safety. Refer to Section 7.7.1 for details.
L135: ## 3.1.2.2. Impact of the Lack of Run-In Period on the Assessment of Safety
L136: The Applicant did not include a run-in period in the phase 3 trials as recommended by the Agency to identify the study population with an inadequate response to topical corticosteroids (TCS). However, per inclusion criteria, enrollment included subjects who had a documented recent history of inadequate response to treatment with TCS (at any time within 1 year before the screening visit) or for whom TCS are documented to be otherwise medically inadvisable (e.g. due to important side effects or safety risks). In addition, inclusion criteria specified enrollment of subjects who were adherent to standard non-medicated skin care including avoidance of known and relevant irritants and allergens. Also, enrollment included subjects who discontinued daily TCS no earlier than 2, 4, or 8 weeks prior to screening, compared with subjects who used daily TCS on the hands after screening, up to 2 weeks before baseline (“run-in-like” exposure). There was no difference in adverse events between subjects with or without TCS “run-in-like” periods (Refer to Section 7.7.2 for details).
L137: ## 3.1.2.3. Evaluation of JAK Inhibitor Class Safety Labeling for Inflammatory Conditions in Context of the Delgocitinib Development Program
L138: JAK inhibitors for the treatment of inflammatory conditions include a Box Warning for serious infections, increased all-cause mortality, malignancy and lymphoproliferative disorders, MACE, and thrombosis. The size of the safety database for delgocitinib was not sufficiently large and long to exclude all the above adverse events. However, during the development program adverse events of neutropenia and lymphopenia as well as serious infections (EH and HZ) were observed. The labeling will include potential risks associated with JAKi class in Warnings and Precautions section. In addition, the Applicant will be required to conduct a well-controlled clinical trial to further evaluate adverse events of special interest (as PMR.) A discussion of labeling is included in Sections 7.2 and 7.7.3 of this review.
L139: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L140: The Applicant submitted data from 15 clinical trials to support approval of delgocitinib cream for the topical treatment of moderate to severe CHE in adults who have had an inadequate response
L141: ## NDA 219155
## ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L142: to, or for whom topical corticosteroids are not advisable. These trials included seven completed clinical trials in subjects with CHE, including:
L143: - Two 16-week vehicle-controlled phase 3 trials (LP0133-1401/DELTA 1 [n =487] and LP0133-1402/DELTA 2 [n =473]) in adult subjects with moderate to severe CHE
- A 36-week open-label single-arm extension phase 3 trial (LP0133-1403/DELTA 3 [n =801]) in subjects with moderate to severe CHE continuing from Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
- A 24-week phase 3, randomized, assessor-blinded active comparator, parallel-group, 2-arm trial (LP0133-1528 DELTA FORCE) to compare the efficacy and safety of delgocitinib cream, 20 mg/g twice daily with alitretinoin capsules once daily in 513 adult subjects with severe CHE
- A phase 1 pharmacokinetic (PK) trial (LP0133-2285) in 16 adult subjects with moderate to severe CHE
- A phase 2 dose-ranging trial (LP0133-1273) in 258 subjects with mild to severe CHE
- A phase 2 proof of concept trial (LP0133-1180) in 91 subjects with at least mild CHE
L144: In addition, the Applicant submitted data from trials that evaluated the effects of delgocitinib cream on dermal safety (LP0133-1408 and -1411), the potential for QT interval (QT) prolongation (LP0133-1409), and in different populations/indications. The Applicant provided supportive data from two trials in subjects with atopic dermatitis (AD) (LP0133-1181 and -1275). Trial LP0133-1181 was an 8-week, phase 1, open-label, multicenter, single-arm trial to evaluate the safety and pharmacokinetics of twice daily topical delgocitinib under maximal use conditions (MuST) in 46 adults, adolescents, and children with moderate to severe AD. Trial LP0133-1275 was an 8-week, phase 2b, double-blind, randomized, 5-arm, vehicle-controlled dose-ranging trial to evaluate delgocitinib cream 1, 3, 8, and 20 mg/g compared to vehicle in 250 adult subjects with mild to severe AD.
L145: The review team focused the evaluation of the safety and efficacy of delgocitinib cream on data from the two vehicle-controlled phase 3 trials (DELTA 1 and DELTA 2) and the open-label extension trial (DELTA 3) that evaluated subjects with moderate to severe CHE. Data from the phase 2 dose-ranging trial (LP0133-1273) was not pooled with the data from the phase 3 trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 because of differences in study design and study population. The study population was defined using a different scale and had different disease severity (mild to severe CHE) than phase 3 trials. In addition, Trial LP0133-1273 (and trials LP0133-1181, 1273, 1275, and 1408) evaluated a formulation of delgocitinib cream that included a different concentration of [REDACTED] (b) (4).
L146: Assessment of the data for subjects with moderate to severe CHE enrolled in Trial LP0133-1273 and DELTA FORCE and subjects with AD identified no unique safety signals (refer to Section 7.6.2 of this review).

L167: ## 4. Patient Experience Data
L168: There were no patient-focused drug development (PFDD) meetings exploring the impact of CHE on patients. However, CHE is frequently associated with an atopic diathesis. Therefore, there are relevant comments from the PFDD meeting exploring the social and economic burdens of AD that was held on September 23, 2019. In collaboration with the U.S. FDA, five groups that are serving the AD community (Allergy &amp; Asthma Network, Asthma and Allergy Foundation of America, Global Parents for Eczema Research, International Topical Steroid Awareness Network, and National Eczema Association) organized the meeting to address the burdens associated with AD and the challenges of managing the disease. Because AD is primarily a pediatric disorder, this meeting did not focus on the effects of this skin disease on the ability to work and other challenges experienced by adults with hand eczema related to their activities of daily living.
L169: Patients shared concerns regarding the appearance of their skin, symptoms of pruritus and pain, recurrent infections, loss of sleep, social isolation, depression/anxiety and financial burdens. Per the respondents to the *More Than Skin Deep* survey conducted at the PFDD meeting, 79% of patients identified itch as the most problematic symptom of AD. Patients described itch as contributing to skin damage and physical harm, shame, difficulty with mood and attention, negative effects on social and intimate relationships, poor school and work performance, negative self-image, depression, and anxiety. An adult attendee stated, “I still wake up unconsciously clawing at my skin. When I itch during the day, I can find a distraction or I can try and control it, but there’s no control over scratching in my sleep.”
L170: In addition, patients reported that the appearance of the skin is the second most burdensome aspect of AD that led to numerous negative impacts on daily life, including low mood, poor self-image, lack of confidence, discomfort, and pain. One patient stated, “At age 26, I had the worst flare of my life. I became unrecognizable, being covered from my scalp to my legs, with all my hair falling out as well. During this time, I would wake up in the morning with instant anxiety.”
L171: Patients with AD who attended the meeting described serious impacts on mental health, including anxiety and depression, and social interactions. A recurring theme was the oppressive, omnipresent influence of AD on all aspects of their lives. One patient shared, “I got depressed. I was suicidal. I couldn’t look at myself in the mirror because I didn’t control who I was. [Eczema] was controlling me.” The meeting also highlighted the challenges with interacting and maintaining relationships with friends, family, and strangers. One AD patient at the meeting shared, “My social life is nonexistent. Something as simple as going to my best friend’s house had to be cut because she had a cat. Every relationship has been completely transformed. Even going to the beach or the pool creates fear or simply avoidance because of the unknown effects on this disease.”
L172: The meeting also described the economic burden of AD, including medical costs and costs from lost work or school. One meeting participant shared, “My skin was so bad that I ended up in the hospital for weeks at a time with severe staph infections. That obviously caused me to miss endless days of school, and then even work further on in life.”
L173: The meeting showed that the burden of treating AD was almost as great as the burden associated with the disease itself. Overall, a vast majority, 89% of meeting participants and survey respondents, reported limited treatment effectiveness and/or harms from current therapies.
L174: This PFDD meeting underscored the large number of unmet medical needs among people affected by AD. When patients were asked about the ideal treatment benefit, “immediate and sustained relief from itch” received the greatest number of responses by polling and was selected by 51% of survey respondents. The second most widely endorsed treatment benefit was “increased ability to go about daily life,” with 15% of survey respondents selecting it. One AD patient shared, “I want to have a medication that lets us eczema warriors live our lives fully and not just exist through the next flare.” Many people expressed a desire for more targeted approaches to therapy based on biomarkers, clinical features, and/or treatment goals and a need for more evidence to inform treatment decisions, especially for children. One AD patient stated, “We need different types of treatments because not everyone is the same.”

L178: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L179: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L180: The nonclinical data support the potential effectiveness of delgocitinib based on the following findings:
L181: - Delgocitinib exhibited inhibitory activity against human JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2 in a kinase assay.
- Delgocitinib inhibited the phosphorylation of STATs stimulated by cytokines interleukin (IL)-2, IL-6, granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor (GM-CSF), and IL-23 in human peripheral-blood-derived mononuclear cells.
- Delgocitinib inhibited human T cell and B cell proliferation.
- Delgocitinib inhibited IL-13 secretion by mast cells stimulated with IL-4 in an IgE-crosslinking setting in human cord-blood-derived mononuclear cells.
- Delgocitinib inhibited tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-\alpha production in monocytes isolated from human peripheral blood in response to GM-CSF stimulation.
- Delgocitinib reversed the IL-4 and IL-13-mediated reduced expression of skin-barrier-related genes in primary human keratinocytes.
- Oral delgocitinib suppressed skin inflammation in mice with 2,4-dinitrofluorobenzene-induced dermatitis.
- Topical delgocitinib suppressed skin inflammation in rats with 2,4-dinitrochlorobenzene-induced dermatitis.
- Topical delgocitinib improved skin barrier function in a dry skin mouse model.
- Oral and topical delgocitinib suppressed IL-31-induced scratching behavior in mice.
L182: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology / Pharmacokinetics
L183: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L184: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitor
Mechanism of action	Delgocitinib, a JAK inhibitor, inhibits the activity of JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and tyrosine kinase 2 (TYK2). JAK signaling involves recruitment of signal transducers and activators of transcription (STATs) to cytokine receptors, and activation and subsequent localization of STATs to the nucleus, leading to the expression of cytokine-responsive genes to induce specific biological responses in target cells. The exact mechanism of action of delgocitinib in the treatment of moderate to severe CHE is currently not known.
Active moieties	Delgocitinib
QT prolongation	No QTc interval prolongation based on concentration-QTc analysis.
General Information
Bioanalysis	An HPLC-MS/MS method for quantification of delgocitinib was developed and validated over a range of 5 to 5000 pg/mL with an LLOQ of 5 pg/mL. The method was successfully used to determine delgocitinib in the in vivo studies.
Healthy subjects versus patients	Pharmacokinetics were determined in subjects with moderate to severe chronic hand eczema. No PK assessment in healthy subjects was conducted.

Absorption
Bioavailability	Absolute bioavailability of delgocitinib cream, 2% was not determined; however, the systemic exposure of delgocitinib cream under maximal use conditions in subjects with CHE was found to be lower than the oral dose used in the TQT study.

Distribution
Volume of distribution	Not applicable
Plasma protein binding	Not applicable
Drug as substrate of transporters	In vitro studies showed that delgocitinib is a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp).
Elimination
Mass balance results	Mass balance study was not conducted.
Clearance	Not applicable
Half-life	Approximately 21 hours following twice-daily dosing for 8 days based on data from a limited number of subjects
Metabolic pathway(s)	Limited metabolism of delgocitinib was observed in vitro and in vivo, with no major human-specific metabolites. Metabolism occurred by oxidation and through glucuronide conjugation. Delgocitinib was mainly metabolized by Cytochrome P450 (CYP) A4/5, with minor contribution from CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP2D6.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	Approximately 75% of the total delgocitinib was excreted unchanged in urine.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	No studies were conducted to determine the therapeutic individualization based on body weight.
Age	No studies were conducted to establish safety and efficacy in pediatric patients. Based on the clinical trials, no overall differences in safety or effectiveness were observed between subjects 65 years of age and older and younger adult subjects.
Renal impairment	Studies in subjects with renal impairment were not conducted due to low systemic exposures and the lack of a relationship between plasma concentration and adverse events, including adverse events of special interest (AESIs).
Hepatic impairment	Studies in subjects with hepatic impairment were not conducted due to low systemic exposures and the lack of a relationship between plasma concentration and adverse events, including AESIs.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Based on in vitro studies, delgocitinib is not an inhibitor of CYP enzymes including CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP3A4, or CYP3A5, and it is not an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Based on in vitro studies, delgocitinib is a substrate of P-gp but not a substrate of BCRP and not an inhibitor of P-gp or BCRP. It is a weak substrate of OCT2 and OAT3, but not a substrate of OAT1 and showed no inhibition of OCT2, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3.

L191: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L192: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L193: The Applicant conducted two phase 3 trials (DELTA 1 [LP0133-1401] and DELTA 2 [LP0133-1402]). The proposed dosing regimen was determined to be acceptable for approval.
L194: The dosing regimen of twice-daily delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g, administered in Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, was selected based on the results from a dose-ranging trial (LP0133-1273). Trial LP0133-1273 was a phase 2b double-blind, multicenter, randomized, 5-arm, vehicle-controlled, parallel-group trial, designed to establish dose response and to investigate the efficacy and safety of twice-daily topical application of delgocitinib cream for 16 weeks in adult subjects with mild to severe CHE. A total of 258 subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1:1:1 ratio to 4 different strengths of delgocitinib cream (1, 3, 8, or 20 mg/g) or vehicle cream.
L195: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L196: ## 6.2.1. Trial Design, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L197: Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 were randomized, double-blind, vehicle-controlled, parallel-group, multicenter, phase 3 trials to evaluate the safety and efficacy of twice-daily delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g compared with vehicle cream in adult subjects with moderate to severe chronic hand eczema.
L198: Figure 1 and Figure 2 present the trial design schematics for Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, respectively. Trial DELTA 1 was designed to enroll and randomize approximately 470 subjects. Trial DELTA 2 was designed to enroll and randomize approximately 450 subjects. The protocols for both trials specified enrolling subjects from approximately 55 sites in Europe and Canada. Subjects were specified to be randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g or vehicle cream. The protocols specified stratifying the randomization by region and baseline IGA-CHE score.
L199: The first application of investigational medicinal product (IMP) was specified to be administered at the trial site at baseline (Day 1). The subsequent IMP administrations were performed by the subjects at home twice daily for 16 weeks. For both trials, subjects were scheduled to have the following trial site visits: screening (Day -28 to -7), baseline (Day 1), and Weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, and 16. The protocols specified that all eligible subjects who did not permanently discontinue IMP prior to Week 16 would be invited to participate in the long-term extension trial (LP0133-1403; DELTA 3).
L200: The protocols specified that, if deemed to be medically necessary by the investigator (i.e., to control intolerable CHE symptoms), rescue treatment for CHE could be prescribed to trial subjects at the discretion of the investigator. The investigators were instructed to make every
L201: NDA 219155
L202: ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L203: attempt to conduct efficacy and safety assessments (e.g., disease severity scores, safety laboratory assessments) immediately before administering any rescue treatment. The protocols specified that if rescue treatment was initiated, the subject was to stop treatment with IMP immediately and would not be allowed to restart treatment with IMP. Subjects who discontinued IMP were specified to be asked to attend an early termination visit, a follow-up visit (performed via phone, but can be a site visit if needed) 2 weeks after the last application of IMP, and also the primary endpoint visit at Week 16.
L204: The protocols stated:
L205: “Rescue treatment is defined as treatment initiated to treat intolerable CHE symptoms during the treatment and follow-up periods. It will be recorded in the eCRF if a medication is given as rescue treatment.”

L208: Abbreviations: d, day; IGA-CHE, Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema; LTE, long-term extension; N, number of subjects; w, week
L209: Figure 2. Trial Design Schematic for Trial DELTA 2

L212: ## 6.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L213: Key eligibility criteria for both trials are summarized in this section and the full criteria are available in Table 127 and Table 128 in Section 15.
L214: ## Inclusion Criteria
L215: - Age 18 years or older at screening.
- Clinical diagnosis of CHE, defined as hand eczema that has persisted for more than 3 months or returned twice or more within the last 12 months.
- Disease severity graded as moderate to severe at screening and baseline according to IGA-CHE (i.e., an IGA-CHE score of 3 or 4).
- Hand eczema symptom diary itch score (weekly average) of \ge 4 points at baseline. The baseline weekly average was to be calculated from daily assessments of itch severity during the 7 days immediately preceding the baseline visit (Day -7 to Day -1). A minimum of 4 itch scores out of the 7 days was required to calculate the baseline average score.
- Subjects who had a documented recent history of inadequate response to treatment with TCS (at any time within 1 year before the screening visit) or for whom TCS were documented to be otherwise medically inadvisable (e.g., due to important side effects or safety risks).Inadequate response was defined as a history of failure to achieve and maintain a low disease activity state (comparable to an IGA-CHE score of \le 2) despite treatment with a daily regimen of TCS of class III-IV (potent to very potent) for Europe and class IV-I
L216: (medium potency to very/ultra-high potency) for Canada, applied for at least 28 days or for the maximum duration recommended by the product prescribing information, whichever was shorter.
L217: - Important side effects or safety risks were those that outweighed the potential treatment benefits and included intolerance to treatment, hypersensitivity reactions, and significant skin atrophy as assessed by the physician.
L218: ## **Exclusion Criteria**
L219: - Concurrent skin diseases on the hands, e.g., tinea manuum.
- Active AD requiring medical treatment in regions other than the hands and feet.
- Active psoriasis on any part of the body.
- Hyperkeratotic hand eczema in combination with a history of psoriasis on any part of the body.
- Clinically significant infection (e.g., impetiginized hand eczema) on the hands.

L228: ## 6.2.4. Clinical Outcome Assessment Instruments Used To Assess the Primary and Secondary Endpoints, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L229: The primary and secondary COA-based efficacy endpoints in Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 included scores from the IGA-CHE. Key COA-based secondary efficacy endpoints included scores from the HESD, HECSI, and the HEIS. COAs used in these endpoints are described below. The IGA-CHE and HESD itch and pain scores were determined to be valid (i.e., fit-for-purpose) and interpretable to evaluate the effects of delgocitinib on subjects' symptoms.
L230: ## 6.2.4.1. Investigator's Global Assessment for Chronic Hand Eczema
L231: The IGA-CHE (Table 6) is a single-item ClinRO designed to assess CHE severity using a 5-level response scale ranging from 0 (clear) to 4 (severe), with higher scores representing greater CHE severity. The recall period is the subject's current state. In Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, the IGA-CHE was administered at Screening (Day -28 to -7), Baseline (Day 1), and Weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, and 16.
L232: **Table 6. Investigator's Global Assessment for Chronic Hand Eczema Scale**
L233: IGA-CHE severity	IGA-CHE score	Sign and intensity
Clear	0	No signs of erythema, scaling, hyperkeratosis/lichenification, vesiculation, oedema or fissures
Almost clear	1	Barely perceptible erythema
No signs of scaling, hyperkeratosis/ lichenification, vesiculation, oedema or fissures
Mild	2	At least one:Slight but definite erythema (pink)
Slight but definite scaling (mostly fine scales)
Slight but definite hyperkeratosis/lichenification
and at least one:Scattered vesicles, without erosion
Barely palpable oedema
Superficial fissures

Moderate	3	At least one:Clearly perceptible erythema (dull red)
Clearly perceptible scaling (coarse scales)
Clearly perceptible hyperkeratosis/lichenification
and at least one:Clustered vesicles, without visible erosion
Definite oedema
Definite fissures

Severe	4	At least one:Marked erythema (deep or bright red)
Marked and thick scaling
Marked hyperkeratosis/lichenification
and at least one:High density of vesicles with erosions
Marked oedema
One or more deep fissures
L234: Source: page 73 of the protocols (Version 4.0) for Trials LP0133-1401 and LP0133-1402
L235: Abbreviation: IGA-CHE, Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema
L236: ## 6.2.4.2. Hand Eczema Severity Index
L237: The HECSI is a ClinRO designed to assess the extent of hand eczema on each of five hand regions (fingertips, fingers [except fingertips], palms of hands, backs of hands, and wrists) and the severity of six clinical signs (erythema, infiltration/papulation, vesicles, fissures, scaling, oedema) for each of five hand regions.
L238: First, the investigator assesses the relative percentage of hand eczema involvement for the given hand region (total of both hands, e.g., 10 fingers). Each region is assessed separately and scored from 0 to 100%. The percentage of involvement of the given hand region is converted to a 5-level response scale representing the area score for both hands together (Table 7) (skin lesions on the hands not representing eczema are not to be included in the assessment). Second, the investigator rates the average severity of each of the 6 clinical signs of hand eczema for the given hand region using a 4-level response scale ranging from 0 ("none/absent") to 3 ("severe") (Table 7). These two steps are repeated for all 5 hand regions. New CHE lesions that occur on previously untreated areas are specified to be included in the assessment.
L239: For each of the hand regions, the region score is calculated by adding up the severity scores for the 6 clinical signs of hand eczema and multiplying that value with the area score (Table 8). The HECSI score equals the sum of the region scores and ranges from 0 (lowest possible score) to
L240: NDA 219155
L241: ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L242: 360 (highest possible score); the severity score is based on both hands, with higher scores indicating more severe and/or more extensive disease. A scoring calculator is available online (HECSI 2025). The recall period is the subject's current state.
L243: In Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, the HECSI was administered at Baseline (Day 1) and Weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, and 16.
L244: **Table 7. HECSI Severity Score Scale and Area Score Scale**
L245: Severity score (SS) scale
(based on both hands)	Area score (AS) scale
(based on the affected area of both hands)
0	None/absent	0	0% affected area
1	Mild	1	1% to 25% affected area
2	Moderate	2	26% to 50% affected area
3	Severe	3	51% to 75% affected area
Note: half-scores (0.5, 1.5, 2.5) are not allowed.	4	76% to 100% affected area
L246: Note: half-scores (0.5, 1.5, 2.5, 3.5) are not allowed.
L247: Source: page 74 of the protocol (Version 4.0) for Trial LP0133-1401 and page 73 of the protocol (Version 4.0) for Trial LP0133-1402
Abbreviations: AS, area score; SS, severity score

Palm of hands	(SS +)	(SS +)	(SS +)	(SS +)	(SS +)	(SS)	× AS

L251: ## 6.2.4.3. Hand Eczema Symptom Diary
L252: The HESD is a 6-item patient-reported outcome (PRO) collected daily with an electronic diary (eDiary) assessing the severity of CHE signs and symptoms (i.e., itch, pain, cracking, redness, dryness, flaking). Subjects rate the sign or symptom at its “worst” on an 11-level response scale,
L253: ## NDA 219155
## ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L254: with higher scores indicating greater severity (0 = “No [sign or symptom]”, 10 = “Severe [sign or symptom]”). At least 4 of 7 days of weekly eDiary data are required to calculate the baseline score and weekly scores. The HESD score is derived as weekly average of the 6 item scores. The recall period is the past 24 hours. See Figure 3 below for a copy of the HESD.
L255: In Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, the HESD was administered electronically daily from Screening (Visit 1; Day -28 to -7) to Week 16. The baseline score is determined based on the average during the 7 days immediately preceding the baseline visit (Day -7 to Day -1).

L266: ## 6.2.4.4. Hand Eczema Impact Scale
L267: The HEIS is a 9-item PRO assessing impacts of CHE over the past 7 days on domains of functioning and health-related quality of life. The HEIS generates a total score and a 3-item PDAL domain score assessing the ability to use soaps/cleaning products, complete housework involving hands getting wet, and wash oneself. Each item is rated on a 5-level response scale ranging from 0 (not at all) to 4 (extremely). A higher score indicates greater impact. The total HEIS score is calculated by taking the average of all 9 items. The HEIS PDAL score is the average of the 3 items in that domain.
L268: See Figure 4 below for a copy of the HEIS PDAL. In Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, the HEIS was administered at Baseline (Day 1) and Weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 16.
