L279: ## 6.2.5. Statistical Analysis Plan, DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 Trials
L280: ## Analysis Populations
L281: The protocols and the statistical analysis plans (SAPs) specified the following analysis populations:
L282: - Full analysis set (FAS): all subjects randomized and exposed to IMP. Subjects were included in the analyses according to the planned treatment.
- Safety analysis set: all subjects exposed to IMP. Subjects were to be included in the analyses according to the treatment they actually received.
L283: The FAS was specified to be the analysis population for efficacy and the safety analysis set was specified to be the analysis population for safety.
L284: ## **Estimands**
L285: The protocols and SAPs specified the following as the main estimand for the primary and binary multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoints:
L286: - Treatment: delgocitinib 20 mg/g and cream vehicle
- Population: FAS
- Endpoint: a binary response at a specified timepoint
- Intercurrent events (ICEs) and strategy for handling:Initiation of rescue treatment: subjects who received rescue treatment prior to the specified timepoint for the endpoint were considered as nonresponders [composite variable strategy]
- Permanent discontinuation of IMP: subjects who permanently discontinue IMP prior to the specified timepoint for the endpoint were considered as nonresponders [composite variable strategy]
- Summary measure: difference in proportions between delgocitinib and vehicle.
L287: The protocols and SAPs specified the following as the main estimand for the continuous multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoints:
L288: - Treatment: delgocitinib 20 mg/g and cream vehicle
- Population: FAS
- Endpoint: absolute change or percent change from baseline to Week 16
- ICEs and strategy for handling:Initiation of rescue treatment: subjects who received rescue treatment prior to Week 16 were to have data after initiation imputed using the worst observation carried forward (WOCF) prior to initiation (including baseline value) [composite variable strategy]
- Permanent discontinuation of IMP: subjects who permanently discontinue IMP prior to Week 16 were to have data after discontinuation imputed using WOCF prior to discontinuation (including baseline value) [composite variable strategy]
- Summary measure: difference in mean change or percentage change from baseline to the endpoint of interest between delgocitinib and vehicle.
L289: ## **Analysis Methods**
L290: The protocols and SAPs specified analyzing the primary and binary secondary efficacy endpoints using the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) test stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE score. The difference in proportions and its 95% CI were specified to be calculated by the Mantel-Haenszel method. Missing data were specified to be imputed using the nonresponder imputation approach.
L291: The protocols and SAPs specified analyzing the continuous secondary efficacy endpoints using an analysis of covariance model with effects of treatment group, region, baseline IGA-CHE score, and baseline value (endpoint of interest). The least square means for each treatment group were specified to be estimated based on the model. In addition, the difference in least square
L292: means between the treatment groups was specified to be estimated along with its corresponding 95% CI. Missing data were specified to be imputed using the WOCF (including baseline value) imputation approach.
L293: ## **Multiplicity Adjustment Plan**
L294: The protocols and SAPs specified using a closed testing procedure with hierarchical tests, alpha splitting, and alpha recycling to control the overall Type I error at a nominal one-sided 2.5% level (equivalent to a two-sided 5% level). Superiority of delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g in terms of IGA-CHE TS at Week 16 (i.e., the primary efficacy endpoint) was the first hypothesis tested at the overall one-sided significance level of 2.5%. In the procedure, if a test was significant, the significance level could be reallocated according to the weight and the direction of the arrows as specified in Figure 5. Each hypothesis was tested at its local significance level (\alpha-local). This process was repeated until no further tests were significant.
L295: **Figure 5. Graphical Display of the Closed Testing Procedure for Primary and Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints**
L296: - From baseline among subjects with a baseline HESD itch score (weekly average) \ge 4 points.
- From baseline among subjects with a baseline HESD score (weekly average) of \ge 4.
- From baseline among subjects with a baseline HESD pain score (weekly average) of \ge 4.
- From baseline among subjects with a baseline DLQI score of \ge 4.
L297: Source: page 108 of the protocol (Version 4.0) for Trial LP0133-1401 and page 104 of the protocol (Version 4.0) for Trial LP0133-1402
L298: Abbreviations: DLQI, Dermatology Life Quality Index; HECSI, Hand Eczema Severity Index; HESD, Hand Eczema Symptom Diary; IGA-CHE, Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema; PDAL, proximal daily activity limitations
L299: ## **Sensitivity and Supplementary Analyses**
L300: The protocols specified the following sensitivity analyses for the primary efficacy endpoint:
L301: ## **Multiple Imputation**
L302: Missing data were imputed from a Bernoulli distribution with parameter p-values 0.1 and 0.2. The same parameter p was used for both treatment groups. The parameter values 0.1 and 0.2 were based on the response rates of IGA-CHE TS and HESD itch score (weekly average)
L303: ≥4 points, respectively, at Week 16 in the cream vehicle group in LP0133-1273 and were to introduce uncertainty due to missing data. A multiple imputation procedure (100 iterations) was used for each value of p. For each of the 100 complete datasets, the difference in response rates were analyzed by the Mantel-Haenszel method stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE score. The results of the analyses were combined using Rubin's rule to provide the estimated treatment difference and associated standard error, and the 95% CI for the treatment difference was calculated.
L304: ## Tipping Point Analysis
L305: For subjects who did not experience ICEs prior to the endpoint of interest, missing data were handled as follows:
L306: - For subjects in the delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g group, missing data were imputed as nonresponse.
- For subjects in the cream vehicle group, missing data were imputed from a Bernoulli distribution with parameter p (ranging from 0 to 1). By varying the parameter p, different percentages of subjects in the cream vehicle group were assumed to be responders, deviating from the default zero percent used in the primary analysis. A multiple imputation procedure (100 iterations) was used for each value of p.
L307: For each of the 100 complete datasets, the difference in response rates was analyzed by the Mantel-Haenszel method stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE score. The results of the analyses were combined using Rubin's rule to provide the estimated treatment difference and associated standard error, and the 95% CI for the treatment difference was calculated. The tipping point was found as the value of p which changed the conclusion from statistically significant to nonsignificant and was judged from a clinical perspective.
L308: The protocols specified the following supplementary analyses for the primary efficacy endpoint.
L309: ## Supplementary Estimand: “Pandemic-Modified Composite” Strategy
L310: This strategy evaluated the treatment effect in adult subjects with moderate to severe CHE, without initiation of rescue treatment or permanent discontinuation of IMP, in a world without the COVID-19 pandemic, where interest lies in the treatment effect as it would have been observed had the COVID-19 pandemic not happened.
L311: - For subjects who experienced ICEs independent of the pandemic prior to the endpoint of interest, observed data after the ICEs were to be considered nonresponse.
- For subjects who experienced an ICE related to the pandemic prior to the endpoint of interest, observed data after the ICE were to be “treated as missing” and imputed assuming missing at random (MAR).
L312: Missing data for subjects who did not attend the visit for the endpoint of interest and did not experience ICEs prior to this visit were to be imputed as nonresponse, unless data were missing due to the pandemic, in which case they were to be imputed assuming MAR. The procedure for imputing values was to be implemented in a 2-step procedure, where all missing or “treated as missing” data were to be imputed using multiple imputation (100 iterations) assuming MAR within treatment groups. Once the 100 complete data sets had been generated by multiple
L313: imputation, nonresponder imputation of relevant data points not affected by the pandemic was to be applied according to the rules described above. For each of the 100 complete datasets, the difference in response rates was to be analyzed using the CMH test stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE score. The results of the analyses were to be combined using Rubin's rule to provide the estimated treatment difference and associated standard error, and the 95% CI for the treatment difference was to be calculated.
L314: ## Supplementary Estimand: “Treatment Policy” Strategy
L315: This strategy evaluated the treatment effect in adult subjects with moderate to severe CHE, regardless of initiation of rescue treatment or permanent discontinuation of IMP, in a world without the COVID-19 pandemic. Observed data were to be used in the analysis, including the data observed after the occurrence of ICEs independent of the pandemic. To support the “treatment policy” strategy, subjects who experienced ICEs prior to Week 16 were to be asked to attend the primary endpoint visit at Week 16 for collection of data. For subjects who experienced an ICE related to the pandemic prior to Week 16, observed data at Week 16 were to be “treated as missing” and imputed assuming MAR.
L316: Missing data at Week 16 independent of the pandemic were to be imputed using multiple imputation (100 iterations) assuming MAR within 4 groups defined according to treatment group and whether the subject had experienced an ICE (independent of the pandemic). Missing data or “treated as missing” data at Week 16 due to the pandemic were to be imputed using multiple imputation assuming MAR within treatment group using data from subjects who had not experienced an ICE. For each of the 100 imputed datasets, the difference in response rates were to be analyzed using the CMH test stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE score. The results of the analyses were to be combined using Rubin's rule to provide the estimated treatment difference and associated standard error, and the 95% CI for the treatment difference was to be calculated.
L317: ## 6.2.6. Results of Analyses, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L318: ## Subject Disposition, Demographics and Baseline Disease Characteristics
L319: Trial DELTA 1 enrolled and randomized a total of 487 subjects (325 to delgocitinib, 162 to vehicle) from 55 sites. Trial DELTA 2 enrolled and randomized a total of 473 subjects (314 to delgocitinib, 159 to vehicle) from 55 sites. Table 9 presents the subject disposition for the vehicle-controlled phase of the trials. In both trials, the treatment and discontinuation rates were higher in the vehicle group compared to the delgocitinib group. In addition, the treatment and trial discontinuation rates were higher in Trial DELTA 2 compared to Trial DELTA 1. The demographics and baseline disease characteristics were generally balanced across the two treatment groups and are presented in Table 10.

L333: ## Rescue Therapy Use

L340: ## Results for the Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L341: Table 12 presents the results of the primary efficacy endpoint (i.e., proportion of subjects with IGA-CHE treatment success at Week 16) in the FAS population for Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2. In both trials, delgocitinib was statistically superior to vehicle on the primary efficacy endpoint (p-values \le0.025). The treatment effect was larger in Trial DELTA 2.

L350: **Table 138** and **Table 139** in Section 16.2 present the numbers and percentages of subjects who had missing data after handling the ICEs (i.e., initiation of rescue treatment and permanent discontinuation) for Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, respectively. The tables also present the results for the sensitivity analyses for the handling of missing data as well as the supplementary analyses. There was only one subject in the delgocitinib group for Trial DELTA 1 and one subject in the vehicle group for Trial DELTA 2 that had missing data to be imputed.
L351: Consequently, in both trials, the results were similar across the primary analysis and the sensitivity analyses for handling missing data. The Applicant's tipping point analysis did not tip (i.e., p-value≥0.05) for any of the iterations evaluated by the Applicant for both trials. Under the extreme case (i.e., subjects with missing data in the delgocitinib group imputed as nonresponders and subjects with missing data in the vehicle group imputed as responders), delgocitinib remained significantly superior to vehicle (p-values &lt;0.05) in both trials. The sensitivity analyses for the primary estimand, including the tipping point analysis, supported the conclusion of the primary analysis. In addition, the analyses of the first supplementary estimand (i.e., pandemic-modified composite) and the second supplementary estimand (i.e., treatment policy) also led to the same conclusion as the primary analysis of the primary estimand (i.e., composite) in both trials.

L390: ## Subgroup Analyses
L391: Results for subgroup analyses by age (&lt;65 and \ge65 years), sex, race, ethnicity, region, and baseline IGA score are presented in Table 140 to Table 141 in Section 16.3. For Trial DELTA 1, the treatment effect was higher in males and in the North America subgroup for region. However, for Trial DELTA 2, the treatment effect was generally consistent across the subgroups for sex and region. For race and ethnicity, the sample sizes for some of the subgroups were relatively small; therefore, it would be difficult to detect any differences in efficacy between these subgroups and their complements.
L392: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L393: ## 6.3.1. Impact of the Lack of Run-In Period on the Assessment of Efficacy
L394: ## Issue
L395: The phase 3 trials (DELTA 1 and DELTA 2) did not include a TCS run-in period to identify and randomize subjects with inadequate response to treatment with TCS.
L396: ## Background
L397: At the End-of-Phase 2 meeting, the Agency advised that an inadequate response to TCS therapy may not be reliably determined based on medical history alone. To address this, the Agency recommended incorporating a run-in period with standard care measures (e.g., moisturizers, irritant avoidance) and TCS therapy to prospectively confirm inadequate response.
L398: ## NDA 219155
## ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L399: The Applicant, however, did not incorporate a run-in-period and expressed concern that such a run-in period would unnecessarily re-expose subjects to therapies previously deemed ineffective and delay access to an investigational product in a setting with limited treatment options. In their NDA submission, the Applicant further argued that defining inadequate response based on a single short course of TCS with limited follow-up does not appropriately reflect the chronic, relapsing nature of CHE. The Applicant noted that while patients may experience temporary improvement with a short course of potent TCS, the durability of response, timing of relapse, need for retreatment, and cumulative treatment burden remain uncertain. Therefore, the Applicant concluded that selection based on documented medical history was more reflective of the target patient population.
L400: ## **Assessment**
L401: Although the Applicant did not incorporate a run-in-period into trial design as recommended by the Agency, in response to Agency concerns regarding appropriate definition of the study population, the Applicant analyzed data under TCS run-in-like conditions within the phase 3 trials (comparing subjects who discontinued daily TCS no earlier than 2, 4, or 8 weeks prior to screening, and subjects who used daily TCS on the hands after screening, up to 2 weeks before baseline). The Applicant conducted post hoc subgroup analyses of the primary and key secondary endpoints at Week 16 by these TCS run-in-like periods.
L402: **Table 14** presents the results for the Applicant's post hoc analysis for the primary efficacy endpoint (i.e., proportion of subjects with IGA-CHE treatment success at Week 16) by the TCS run-in-like periods. In Trial DELTA 1, the treatment effect was similar for TCS run-in-like subgroups. In Trial DELTA 2, the treatment effect was slightly smaller for subjects in the TCS run-in-like subgroup than those not in the TCS run-in-like subgroup; however, there was considerable overlap in the 95% CIs.

L410: ## Conclusion
L411: The treatment effect in subjects with and without TCS run-in-like period was generally similar to the overall population. Therefore, the impact of the lack of a formal run-in period on the assessment of efficacy appears low.
L412: ## 6.3.2. Impact of Content Validity, Other Measurement Properties, and Data Interpretability for COA-Based Efficacy Endpoints
L413: ## 6.3.2.1. Fitness for Purpose and Interpretability of the HECSI
L414: ## Issue
L415: There was uncertainty regarding whether the HECSI was fit-for-purpose and interpretable (b)(4) (b)(4).
L416: ## Background
L417: The HECSI is a ClinRO that is a widely used assessment in clinical practice and clinical trials (Rönsch et al. 2019) to evaluate the severity and extent of patients' CHE symptoms on the hands and wrists. The Applicant included the HECSI as it is intended to measure both the extent and the intensity of the disease based on multiple clinical signs. Published evidence indicates the HECSI demonstrates adequate internal consistency (Janstrup et al. 2023) and inter-rater and intra-rater reliability in the assessment of hand eczema (Held et al. 2005).
L418: ## Assessment
L419: The Applicant did not provide justification for the inclusion of each of the six CHE signs (erythema, infiltration/papulation, vesicles, fissures, scaling, edema) in the HECSI. Evidence was not provided to address whether each of the six CHE signs were clinically equivalent to warrant equal weighting in the total HECSI score. Although the HECSI measures clinical signs that are broadly relevant to CHE, the overall score may not validly represent the severity of the hand
L420: eczema. The clinical signs may not be relevant for all patients within or between CHE subtypes leading to over- or underestimation of clinical severity. For this reason, the overall score is not considered a valid representation of CHE severity and cannot be clearly interpreted.
L421: The Applicant's psychometric analysis report indicated weaker internal reliability (&lt;0.70) for both the total HECSI score (incorporating the area calculation) (0.62) and for the score with the area calculation removed (0.54). There was an indication that this scale was not unidimensional, and thus, that the score could not be meaningfully interpreted to reliably characterize the concept of interest (i.e., severity and extent of hand eczema). Combined with the instrument's complex scoring algorithm, which could result in imprecise or inaccurate scores, there remains a concern that scores did not clinically characterize CHE signs such that they were clearly interpretable. Differences in the proportion of subjects achieving &gt;75% (HECSI-75) and &gt;90% (HECSI-90) improvement at Week 16 from baseline cannot be interpreted because the metric is an insufficient indicator of responsiveness, and scores may not adequately reflect CHE severity due to the reasons above.
L422: Despite the limitations discussed above, for completeness, the Agency requested the Applicant conduct quantitative anchor-based analyses using data from Trial DELTA 1 and Trial DELTA 2 to aid in the interpretation of the HECSI-based endpoint scores. Based on these analyses, the Applicant proposed a range of meaningful change thresholds of 50 to 90% improvement in HECSI-based endpoint scores. The Agency reviewed the Applicant's anchor-based analyses and concluded a plausible range of meaningful change values would be a 69 to 82% improvement in HECSI-based endpoint scores. Given the issues with the interpretability of the HECSI score itself (i.e., did not clinically characterize the severity and extent of hand eczema), these meaningful change analyses based on the endpoint scores have limited interpretability. Refer to Section 16.1.6 for details of the FDA's evaluation of meaningful within-subject percent change in the HECSI scores.
L423: ## Conclusion
L424: The factors above render the HECSI not fit-for-purpose for the context of use and the HECSI-based endpoint scores uninterpretable (b) (4).
L425: ## 6.3.2.2. Fitness for Purpose and Interpretability of the HEIS PDAL Domain
L426: ## Issue
L427: There was uncertainty regarding whether the HEIS PDAL domain was fit-for-purpose to support labeling claims.
L428: ## Background
L429: The HEIS is a PRO that assesses the impact of CHE on key aspects of functioning and health-related quality of life. The domain score (b) (4) (PDAL) consists of three items evaluating the impact of hand eczema on subjects' ability to 1) use soaps/cleaning products; 2) complete housework that involves getting their hands wet; and 3) wash themselves. The Applicant completed concept elicitation and cognitive debriefing research to evaluate the content validity of the HEIS. Most subjects understood items within the HEIS, although a few had
L430: difficulty understanding questions related to impacts to work, the use of soaps/cleaning products, and disliking the appearance of the hands. Most subjects found the questions to be relevant (e.g., dislike of appearance, embarrassment, self-consciousness, soap/cleaning products). However, touching objects, wearing gloves, and work were reportedly less relevant, based on CHE type and physician-rated CHE severity.
L431: Items comprising the HEIS PDAL domain were evaluated with subjects (“ability to use products,” “hard to do housework,” and “hard to wash yourself”), and modified to integrate subject feedback. It was concluded that patients would likely understand and use the recall period of the “past 7 days” appropriately within the context of a clinical trial.
L432: ## Assessment
L433: While the development of the HEIS broadly aligned with good COA development guidelines, multiple functional impacts measured by the HEIS co-occur with other dermatologic disease and are therefore not specific to CHE. In the score, equal weighting may not reflect daily life impacts of CHE, and such functional effects may vary by occupational contexts (e.g., washing soap may be more relevant for healthcare workers, household cleaning may be more relevant for caregivers or parents, etc.).
L434: The HEIS PDAL domain comprised items that may be influenced by factors external to the clinical trial. For example, items comprising the scale assess the use of soaps/cleaning products, housework that involves the hands getting wet, and washing oneself. As irritants can exacerbate CHE symptoms, it is not possible to disentangle or control for material (i.e., product-related), environmental (e.g., hard water, water temperature, exposure to other chemicals), or behavioral (e.g., length of exposure, avoidance behavior as noted in the qualitative research stated above) factors that could influence scores on the HEIS.
L435: ## Conclusion
L436: The HEIS PDAL domain was not considered fit-for-purpose (b) (4).
L437: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L438: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L439: The nonclinical safety profile of delgocitinib was evaluated in safety pharmacology studies in rats and dogs (oral); repeat-dose toxicity studies in mice (dermal; 3 months), rats (oral; up to 6 months), dogs (oral; up to 9 months), and minipigs (dermal; up to 9 months); genotoxicity studies; reproductive and developmental toxicity studies in rats and rabbits; juvenile animal toxicity studies in rats and minipigs; and carcinogenicity studies in mice (dermal) and rats (oral). Additional studies included a phototoxicity study in mice, primary skin irritation studies in rabbits and minipigs, a skin sensitization study, a skin photosensitization study and a dermal phototoxicity study in guinea pigs, and a bovine corneal opacity and permeability (BCOP) test. The results from these studies are summarized below.
L440: ## **Safety Pharmacology**
L441: Delgocitinib had no effects on cardiovascular, respiratory, or neurological parameters at clinically relevant doses. Delgocitinib had no remarkable inhibitory effect on the human ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) current at clinically relevant concentrations. Delgocitinib had an inhibitory effect on gastrointestinal transport (↓27%) in rats at an oral dose of 30 mg/kg.
L442: ## **General Toxicology**
L443: No treatment-related changes were observed in a 9-month dermal toxicity study in minipigs treated with delgocitinib ointment. The no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) was 5% (20 mg/kg/day) delgocitinib ointment, the maximum feasible dose. Skin lesions were observed in a 9-month oral toxicity study in dogs at 3.0 mg/kg/day and the dose was lowered to 1.5 mg/kg/day from Week 24. The NOAEL was 0.6 mg/kg/day.

L449: ## **Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology and Juvenile Animal Toxicology**
L450: Reproductive and developmental toxicology studies included fertility and early embryonic development studies in male and female rats, embryo-fetal development studies in rats and rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development study in rats. Adverse effects were seen at higher doses in each of the reproductive and developmental toxicology studies, but a NOAEL was established in each study with acceptable margins of exposures.
L451: Juvenile animal studies were conducted in rats (oral) and minipigs (dermal). There were no changes suggestive of systemic or target organ toxicity at up to the highest dose level, 30 mg/kg/day, in juvenile rats. In juvenile minipigs, there were no treatment-related findings following 28-day repeated dermal application of delgocitinib ointment 5% (20 mg/kg/day).

L458: ## **Genetic Toxicity**
L459: Delgocitinib was not mutagenic in the Ames assay. Delgocitinib had the potential to induce polyploidy in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, but not chromosomal structural aberrations in vitro. Delgocitinib did not induce chromosomal aberrations (structural or numerical aberrations) in rat bone marrow cells in vivo. Delgocitinib ointment (5%) did not induce micronuclei in the epidermal cells of hairless mice.
L460: ## **Carcinogenicity**
L461: In the dermal carcinogenicity study in mice, delgocitinib ointment (1%, 3% and 5%) was administered once daily for 104 weeks. No drug-related neoplastic findings were noted at topical doses up to 5% delgocitinib ointment. In the 2-year oral carcinogenicity study in rats, increased incidence of benign thymoma was noted in females at 30 mg/kg/day (see Section 13.2 for detailed information).

L468: ## **Special Toxicology**
L469: In minipigs, delgocitinib ointment was determined to be nonirritating after 24-hour exposure. The skin sensitization potential of vehicle and 3% delgocitinib ointment was evaluated in guinea pigs and no sensitization was observed. Phototoxicity was evaluated for delgocitinib ointment in guinea pigs, and no effects were observed. Delgocitinib ointment was evaluated in a BCOP test and was found to have no ocular irritant properties. Delgocitinib was immunotoxic in a 4-week oral dose study in rats; however, this is an expected effect because of the pharmacological/immunosuppressive nature of delgocitinib.
L470: Overall, the nonclinical safety assessment supports the approval of this product. ANZUPGO is approvable from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective.
L471: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L472: Janus kinase inhibitors have been associated with a range of serious adverse events, reflected in class labeling including Boxed Warnings and Warnings and Precautions. For JAKi approved to treat inflammatory conditions, Boxed Warnings include serious infections leading to hospitalization or death, including tuberculosis and bacterial, invasive fungal, viral (including herpes zoster reactivation), and opportunistic infections; malignancies, including lymphoma and nonmelanoma skin cancers; increased all-cause mortality including sudden cardiovascular death; MACE, including cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, and stroke; and thrombosis, including deep venous thrombosis, pulmonary embolism, and arterial thrombosis. Such class-wide labeling was informed by a well-controlled long-term safety trial for tofacitinib and is included in labeling for systemic JAKi to treat inflammatory conditions (e.g., baricitinib, upadacitinib tablets) and is included in labeling for the only currently approved topical JAKi (ruxolitinib cream).
L473: ## Considerations for Including a Box Warning and Warnings and Precautions in Labeling as Per Class Labeling for JAKi
L474: ## Measurable Systemic Exposure
L475: Delgocitinib cream, 2% demonstrated measurable systemic absorption in clinical trials (refer to Section 14 of this review). In the maximal use trial LP0133-2285 in 15 subjects with CHE (median BSA 1%, range 0.25 to 3%), mean C_{max} reached nonsubnanomolar levels of 1.53 ng/mL (4.93nM, range 0.03 to 23.52nM) on Day 1 and 0.53 ng/mL (1.71nM, range 0.55 to 3.47nM) on Day 8, with individual subjects reaching concentrations as high as 7.30 ng/mL (23.52nM). In phase 3 trial DELTA 2 (estimated BSA range 0.2 to 4.4%), one subject reached 29.2 ng/mL (94.09nM), and even excluding this outlier, maximum concentrations of 5.65 ng/mL (18.21nM) were observed (refer to Section 14 of this review). The Agency waives certain pharmacology-toxicology and cardiac safety assessments only when systemic exposure under maximal use conditions is subnanomolar (negligible). Systemic exposure to delgocitinib exceeds this threshold for AEs, we cannot exclude the potential of delgocitinib to cause AEs observed with the JAK inhibitors.
L476: ## Unknown Exposure Threshold for Adverse Events
L477: There is no scientifically established systemic exposure threshold below which JAK inhibitor-related adverse events do not occur. The Applicant acknowledges this limitation, stating that "establishing a threshold for systemic ADRs [adverse drug reactions] is not feasible." Given that JAK inhibitors to treat inflammatory conditions as a class have demonstrated serious risks including increased mortality, MACE, thrombosis, and considering that delgocitinib is a pan-JAK inhibitor affecting all four members of the JAK family (JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2), the potential for class-wide effects cannot be dismissed based solely on low exposure levels when the threshold for safety is unknown.
L478: ## Observed Systemic Adverse Reactions and Laboratory Abnormalities
L479: Cytopenias are established adverse reactions associated with JAK inhibitors approved for inflammatory conditions, including neutropenia, lymphopenia, and anemia with baricitinib and upadacitinib, and thrombocytopenia and lymphopenia with abrocitinib.
L480: In vehicle-controlled trials with delgocitinib, 638 subjects received delgocitinib (person-years of observation = 197) and 321 subjects received vehicle (person-years of observation = 94). While it is acknowledged that the unequal exposure would be expected to result in more events in the delgocitinib group, an imbalance in hematologic parameters persists even when accounting for exposure differences through rate calculations: 13.6% of delgocitinib-treated subjects experienced any grade worsening leukocyte shifts compared to 6.2% in the vehicle group (EAIR 44.2 versus 21.3). Neutrophil shifts occurred in 4.4% versus 3.7% and lymphocyte shifts in 2.5% versus 1.9%, respectively. The current exposure-adjusted data reveals a meaningful imbalance that cannot be dismissed given the established mechanistic possibility for hematologic effects with JAK inhibitors. For some subjects, a causal association of hematologic shifts to delgocitinib could not be excluded, and some subjects did not return to pretreatment hematologic values. Refer to Section 7.6.1.6 of this review for individual subject narratives, and Section 17 of this review for comprehensive individual subject analyses of hematologic changes.
L481: Additionally, during the open-label period of the phase 3 trials, two cases of herpes zoster occurred in otherwise healthy subjects less than 50 years of age, and one case of eczema herpeticum was reported in delgocitinib-treated subjects (refer to Section 7.7.3 of this review). While these cases occurred during the open-label period without a concurrent vehicle control for comparison, and the observed rates may be consistent with background incidence rates for herpes zoster and eczema herpeticum, the mechanistic plausibility for a causal relationship cannot be dismissed. JAK inhibitors are known to impair antiviral immune responses by disrupting cytokine signaling pathways critical for NK cell function. NK cells play a central role in early host defense against viral infections, providing biological plausibility that delgocitinib may have contributed to these viral reactivation events.
L482: Postmarketing data from CORECTIM (delgocitinib ointment, 0.5%), which contains the same active moiety, revealed additional immune-related events, including 86 cases of eczema herpeticum and 21 cases of herpes zoster. Notably, events were observed even in pediatric patients as young as 4 and 10 years of age. Important limitations of this postmarketing data include differences in the patient population (atopic dermatitis), product application over larger body surface area, dosage forms and strengths, and lack of a control group for rate comparison. In addition, there is a lack of information regarding the number of patients who used the product and the duration of exposure. Nonetheless, the moiety is the same (delgocitinib), and given the established mechanistic plausibility for JAK-inhibitor-associated immune suppression there is residual uncertainty regarding delgocitinib's potential association with these systemic immune-related adverse events. Refer to Section 7.3 of this review for details.
L483: ## Extent of Safety Database
L484: In the phase 3 clinical trials, less than 700 subjects exposed to delgocitinib in vehicle-controlled trials for 16 weeks and less than 200 subjects with continuous exposure to Week 52. The safety database is not robust enough to exclude that adverse event observed with JAKi products for the treatment of inflammatory conditions.
L485: ## **Applicant Arguments Against a Boxed Warning**
L486: ## **Effective Pan-JAK Inhibition Is Confined to The Skin by the Topical Cream Formulation**
L487: The Applicant stated that ANZUPGO was specifically developed as a topical formulation that allows effective diffusion within the skin compartment to target inflammation locally; the cream is not formulated with “penetration enhancers,” which minimizes transdermal absorption and subsequent systemic circulation exposure. This results in systemic exposure levels that remain lower than levels where meaningful systemic pharmacological effects would occur, contrasted with [REDACTED] (b) (4) which “includes skin penetration enhancers;” the bioavailability of topical delgocitinib cream is 0.6%, approximately an order of magnitude less than [REDACTED] (b) (4) bioavailability.
L488: ## **Review Team Assessment**
L489: Contrary to the Applicant’s assertion that their formulation lacks penetration enhancers, delgocitinib cream [REDACTED] (b) (4). The Applicant’s comparison to [REDACTED] (b) (4) is misleading, as multiple types of penetration enhancers exist, and the presence of [REDACTED] (b) (4) can facilitate dermal penetration and systemic absorption. In addition, the theoretical absence of penetration enhancers would not guarantee safety, particularly given that chronic hand eczema patients may have compromised skin barrier function that could increase absorption unpredictably.
L490: Regarding the Applicant’s argument that the bioavailability of topical delgocitinib cream is 0.6%, approximately an order of magnitude less than [REDACTED] (b) (4) bioavailability, even with 0.6% bioavailability, measurable delgocitinib plasma concentrations were detected in clinical trials, demonstrating that the drug does reach systemic circulation (refer to Section 14 of this review). Comparing bioavailability percentages to another other moiety that has different potency and different JAK inhibition (JAK 1 and JAK2) does not eliminate the fundamental concern about JAK inhibition-related adverse events.
L491: ## **Negligible Level of Systemic Exposure Is Well Characterized by a Highly Sensitive PK Assay**
L492: The Applicant stated: a state-of-the-art “LC-MS/MS” assay was developed with a lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) of 0.005 ng/mL in human plasma; this assay is 200-fold more sensitive than the delgocitinib assay developed by Japan Tobacco (LLOQ of 1 ng/mL) and 60-fold more sensitive than the [REDACTED] (b) (4) assay; the high sensitivity allowed for measurable concentrations in most clinical samples despite negligible systemic exposure.
L493: ## **Review Team Assessment**
L494: There is observed systemic exposure to delgocitinib that is not in subnanomolar range and therefore not considered negligible.
L495: ## **Establishing a Threshold for Systemic ADRs Is Not Feasible, as the Exposure Levels Required for These Effects Cannot Be Reached With the Topical Formulation of Delgocitinib in CHE**
L496: The Applicant acknowledged that an exposure threshold for JAK-inhibitor-related adverse events cannot be established with available data; however, safety margins from nonclinical studies and clinical exposure levels significantly under half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) values in whole blood indicate delgocitinib cream is not expected to have systemic JAK-inhibitor-related effects. Systemic exposure levels with delgocitinib cream are approximately 14-fold lower than the lowest tested oral dose and more than 100-fold lower than IC_{50} values assessed using a whole blood-based assay for ex vivo IL-4 inhibition, and lower than 20% inhibitory concentration (IC_{20}) values for ex vivo interferon (IFN)-\alpha inhibition. No drug-related cases of serious infection, MACE, malignancies, or venous thromboembolic events were observed in clinical trials.
L497: ## **Review Team Assessment**
L498: The Applicant acknowledged that an exposure threshold cannot be established. Without a defined safe exposure threshold level, any systemic exposure represents an unknown risk.
L499: The Applicant's calculations based on IC_{50} and IC_{20} ex vitro values are undermined by actual *clinical* observations. The documented imbalance in hematologic laboratory shifts in the vehicle-controlled trials suggests evidence of systemic pharmacological activity. Furthermore, the company's reliance on ex vitro assays for only 2 cytokines (IL-4 and IFN-\alpha) out of &gt;50 JAK-mediated pathways is insufficient to predict clinical safety. The Applicant's assertion that "systemic exposure levels with delgocitinib cream are approximately 14-fold lower than the lowest tested oral dose" lacks clinical relevance since no oral formulation of delgocitinib is approved for any indication. The referenced "lowest tested oral dose" was administered as a single dose to healthy volunteers in a cardiac safety study (Trial LP0133-1409). This comparison provides no meaningful safety margin assessment for the proposed indication and patient population.
L500: Finally, though no drug-related cases of serious infection, MACE, malignancies, or venous thromboembolic events were observed in clinical trials, the absence of events in clinical trials does not rule out rare but serious adverse events that could occur in larger populations or with longer exposure periods. The safety database is not of sufficient size and duration to reliably exclude adverse events seen with JAK inhibition.
L501: ## **Systemic Exposure to Delgocitinib Sustained at Levels That Do Not Support the Development of Systemic Toxicity**
L502: The Applicant stated: no subjects had continuous delgocitinib exposure levels approaching concentrations sufficient for meaningful systemic JAK inhibition effects. The majority of subjects had plasma concentrations below 1 ng/mL, with no subjects presenting exposure levels above 2 ng/mL continuously throughout trials. Nonclinical studies showed that continuous exposure for at least 3 months at systemic levels around 40 ng/mL is needed before JAK inhibition-related adverse effects are observed. Human exposure of approximately 0.5 ng/mL provides a safety margin in excess of 80-fold.
L503: ## **Review Team Assessment**
L504: The Applicant's argument that no subjects had elevated "continuous" delgocitinib exposure levels fails to account for potential JAK-inhibition-related risks associated with intermittent peak concentrations and cumulative effects over time. In addition, individual patients could experience higher peak concentrations due to factors such as compromised skin barrier function, extensive application areas, or individual pharmacokinetic variability that were not fully captured in the controlled clinical trial setting. Furthermore, the Applicant's extrapolation from nonclinical data showing that 3 months of exposure at 40 ng/mL is needed for adverse effects may not accurately predict human sensitivity to JAK inhibition.
L505: Delgocitinib competitively inhibited JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2 with respect to adenosine triphosphate. The inhibitor constant (K_i) values for delgocitinib on JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2 were 2.08, 1.71, 5.45, and 13.9 nmol/L, respectively. Furthermore, delgocitinib inhibited the JAK activity of JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2 with IC_{50} mean values of 2.8 nmol/L, 2.6 nmol/L, 12.5 nmol/L, and 57.8 nmol/L, respectively. The C_{max} in the maximal use study in subjects with CHE (LP0133-2285) was 1.53 ng/mL (4.93 nmol/L) and 0.53 ng/mL (1.71 nmol/L) on Day 1 and 8, respectively. Overall, the C_{max} is on par with the IC_{50} of JAK1 and JAK2. While the systemic concentration/IC_{50} ratio is much lower than oral JAK inhibitors, the systemic safety concern cannot be ruled out based on the systemic exposure/IC_{50} comparison.
L506: ## **Extensive Postmarketing Safety Data for Exposure to the Same Drug Substance in Japan (CORECTIM) Provide No Evidence of Systemic Adverse Drug Reactions**
L507: The Applicant stated: delgocitinib ointment (CORECTIM) has been approved in Japan for atopic dermatitis since 2020 with an "estimated cumulative exposure of 4,252,624 patients through July 2024." Systemic exposure levels with delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g are similar to those observed with approved CORECTIM in Japan. Postmarketing surveillance has resulted in 15 serious infections from 13 patients among over 4 million exposed patients, with no all-cause mortality, MACE, or thromboembolic events, and one serious malignancy reported. The current CORECTIM package insert does not include risks related to systemic JAK inhibition, indicating that delgocitinib cream does not carry the typical risks associated with the JAK inhibitor boxed warning.
L508: ## **Review Team Assessment**
L509: Given the limitations of the postmarketing data including number of patients exposed, duration of exposure, patient history and laboratory assessment, causality for the reported cases of EH and HZ cannot be established. However, the role of delgocitinib based on its mechanism of action and known adverse reactions (e.g., virus reactivation, serious infection) cannot be excluded.
L510: ## **Combination With Systemic Immunosuppressants**
L511: The use of delgocitinib cream has not been studied in combination with biologic products acting on the immune system within the clinical development program (e.g., therapeutic biologics, other JAK inhibitors, or potent immunosuppressants such as azathioprine or cyclosporine). Such products were specified as prohibited in the trial protocols given the potential to interfere with the clinical efficacy and safety evaluations performed. All JAK inhibitors to treat inflammatory conditions carry a limitation of use for such medications; (b) (4)
L512: "Based on common mechanisms of action shared between systemic immunosuppressants and immunomodulators and topical delgocitinib, additive immunological effects could be expected. However, given the targeted local mechanism of delgocitinib cream with negligible amounts of the drug substance reaching the systemic circulation, any such additive effects would be of primary theoretical concern for the area of application. In terms of efficacy, this could result in an amplification of anti-inflammatory effects and represent an important therapeutic intention of the prescribing clinician. From the safety perspective, while local skin infections have been observed with use of delgocitinib cream, none of them have been severe or treatment-limiting. At most, a caution for the additional use of delgocitinib cream on top of a chosen systemic immunosuppressant or immunomodulator, considering the prescribing information of the individual medication, would be sufficient and empower clinicians to make informed decisions with the maximum ability to treat their patients."
L513: Because delgocitinib was not evaluated for safety in combination with other immunosuppressants, limitation of use section of labeling should include a recommendation that delgocitinib should not be used in combination with other JAKi or potent immunosuppressants.
L514: ## Conclusion
L515: In conclusion, based on the measurable systemic exposure to delgocitinib, absence of established safety threshold of systemic exposure, adverse events observed during clinical trials, and limited safety database, the review team recommends that labeling for delgocitinib cream include the potential risk of adverse reaction related to JAK inhibition in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling. Labeling with a Boxed Warning is not recommended at this time because of the following reasons: 1) the product will be applied to less than 5% BSA in CHE patients, 2) data from the maximum use study showed low systemic exposure (for approximately 33% of the subjects the systemic exposure was sub-nanomolar on Day 1 and for 20% of the subjects it was sub-nanomolar on Day 8); 3) adverse reactions such as thrombosis, malignancy, and MACE were not observed during the development program; and 4) the risk of these major toxicities are dose-dependent. Based on the above, the risk for major toxicities is considered low for the proposed indication.
L516: However, because there were exposures in the nano-molar range and the safety database is of size and duration that cannot reliably exclude potential adverse reactions associated with other JAK inhibitors for the treatment of inflammatory conditions, the labeling will include in the Warnings and Precaution section information regarding the potential risks of JAK inhibition. In addition, to further characterize the long-term safety, the Applicant will be required to conduct a post-marketing active-controlled clinical trial of sufficient size and duration to further characterize safety events of interest.
L517: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L518: Delgocitinib cream, 2% and ointment, 0.5% are approved for marketing in other jurisdictions.
L519: NDA 219155
ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L520: ## **Delgocitinib Cream (ANZUPGO)**
L521: Delgocitinib cream, 2%, under the brand name ANZUPGO is currently approved for use in adults for the treatment of moderate to severe CHE in the European Union (marketing authorization September 19, 2024), United Kingdom, Switzerland, and the United Arab Emirates. Delgocitinib cream is also being evaluated for use in other markets. To date, no patients in the postmarketing database have been treated for more than one year (response to information request dated May 23, 2025). Since the launch of the product to the cutoff date (April 30, 2025), the Applicant received a total of 28 postmarketing safety reports. The duration of exposure from the reports where the information was provided varied from 1 day to 3 months.
L522: The key findings from the available postmarketing data (October 2024 through April 2025) are:
L523: - 28 adverse events (AEs) spontaneously reported (50% of unknown duration)
- 21 AEs of local cutaneous reactions: 8 AEs of hand dermatitis.
- No AEs of thrombosis, neutropenia, leukopenia, or abnormal lipids
L524: ## **Delgocitinib Ointment (CORECTIM)**
L525: Delgocitinib ointment, 0.5%, was initially approved in Japan in January 2020 for the treatment of AD in adults and marketed under the brand name CORECTIM. The population was extended to include patients aged 2 to 16 years (March 2021) and then 6 months to &lt;2 years (January 2023) with approval of delgocitinib ointment, 0.25%.
L526: ## **Exposure**
L527: The Applicant stated that the CORECTIM postmarketing database (January 2020 until April 30, 2025) contains 3,571 case reports “representing use by an estimated 4 million patients between 2020 and January 2025.” Most of these (3,549 of 3,571) case reports described nonserious AEs (response to information request dated May 23, 2025). The Applicant determined the duration of exposure prior to the AE directly from the report or by using the treatment start date and date of the AE. However, in approximately two-thirds of the case reports, the treatment duration is unknown. The Applicant notes that “[t]here are 24 case reports where the duration of exposure prior to onset of the AE is known to exceed 1 year, all of which are nonserious. None of these cases raised any safety concern.” The Applicant further stated, “[e]ven though treatment duration is not available in many cases, it does not undermine the substantiveness of the finding that 4 years of postmarketing safety data from CORECTIM do not reveal any safety concerns.”
L528: The table below provides a summary of the known treatment durations prior to AEs.
L529: **Table 18. Summary of Treatment Duration of CORECTIM Prior to Adverse Events**

Abbreviation: AE, adverse event
L532: ## Adverse Events and Adverse Events of Special Interest
L533: The Applicant submitted an integrated analysis of the cumulative postmarketing safety data for CORECTIM from June 2020 through January 2024. Among 3,446 AEs, the most frequently reported AEs (\ge 1\% of all events) in this analysis by preferred terms (PTs) were: application site erythema (9.6%), application site irritation (5.8%), application site acne (5.6%), application site pruritus (3.4%), application site folliculitis (2.2%), eczema herpeticum (2.2%), and skin irritation (1.7%). Application site acne, folliculitis, herpes simplex, and eczema herpeticum are listed adverse events in the Japanese labeling for CORECTIM.
L534: Regarding infection, there were 86 reports of EH and 21 reports of herpes zoster infection among patients on delgocitinib ointment in the postmarketing setting.
L535: EH is a potentially life-threatening systemic infection caused by the herpes simplex virus (HSV). In the United States, the annual incidence of pediatric hospitalization for EH between 2002 and 2012 ranged from 4 to 7.3 per million children, with risk highest for infants, toddlers, and preschool children (Lee 2025). Risk factors for EH include compromised skin barrier, as seen in conditions such as AD, and immunosuppression due to medications or certain conditions (such as malignancy). About 3% of patients with AD are estimated to develop EH during their lifetime, although EH is a rare occurrence in AD (Damour et al. 2020).
L536: Herpes zoster infection (shingles) is due to reactivation of the varicella-zoster virus. The incidence of HZ varies by age and is approximately two to nine cases per 1,000 people in the U.S. population annually (CDC 2024). Risk factors for HZ include immunosuppression through medications or conditions (such as HIV/AIDS or malignancy), older age (especially age greater than 50), family history of zoster, and physical trauma (Marra et al. 2020). In the postmarketing data for delgocitinib ointment, 0.5%, more than half of the patients with HZ were less than 50 years of age (including a 4-year-old patient and a 10-year-old patient), while two patients had HZ multiple times (twice in the 4-year-old patient and three times in a 19-year-old patient). There was no information on whether HZ was multidermatomal nor any other patient risk factors were provided.
L537: In both EH and HZ, a defect in NK cell function is thought to be involved in the development of both conditions (Goodyear et al. 1996; Kawakami et al. 2017; Della Chiesa et al. 2019), while JAK inhibitors have been associated with NK cell dysfunction (Vian et al. 2019; Meudec et al.
L538: 2023). Therefore, there is biologic plausibility and, in the absence of other risk factors, causality of delgocitinib for these AEs cannot be excluded.
L539: The Applicant searched the postmarketing safety database for adverse events of special interest (AESIs) which included malignancy, thromboembolic events, gastrointestinal (GI) perforation, and MACE. The Applicant identified one malignancy in a female of unknown age who used CORECTIM for AD and developed metastases from breast carcinoma to lymph nodes. The report did not include the temporal relationship of the serious adverse event (SAE) with administration of CORECTIM or the outcome of the event, which led to discontinuation of treatment with CORECTIM. In a search for events of “nonmelanoma skin cancer”, the Applicant identified one report of “skin tumor.” The report specified no other details regarding the event, including the location of the tumor, temporal relationship to the administration of CORECTIM, or action taken with drug. The case was not medically confirmed and was considered as nonserious based on limited available information. To date, the Applicant identified no postmarketing cases of embolic, thrombotic, gastrointestinal perforation, or cardiovascular adverse events in patients treated with CORECTIM.
L540: ## **Laboratory Data and Clinically Significant Shifts**
L541: The Applicant analyzed results from laboratory assessments of complete blood count (CBC) and lipid profiles for patients who underwent testing in the postmarketing surveillance database for ANZUPGO and CORECTIM and related clinically significant shifts. The Applicant stated:
L542: “Postmarketing reports include the clinical data that was volunteered by the reporter and do not necessarily include laboratory assessments, which are not routinely performed or recorded. Postmarketing reports rarely contain pretreatment or post-treatment values, or follow-up values, and some of the assessment dates are missing. Therefore, the postmarketing database is generally a less reliable source than randomized controlled clinical trials for observation of presence or absence of clinically significant shifts. Nevertheless, the reported laboratory assessment results do not raise any safety concerns.”
L543: ## **Hematology**
L544: The postmarketing safety database for CORECTIM identified 14 cases that included test results. Review of these cases by the Applicant showed 6 reports in the CORECTIM database that included tests related to CBC (below) and none related to lipid profiles. Given the limited information overall and the lack of baseline test results, the review team was not able to assess whether any clinically significant laboratory shifts occurred in the postmarketing database.

L558: ## Reports of Adverse Events in the CORECTIM and ANZUPGO Postmarketing Database Related to Abnormal Lipid Parameters
L559: Search of the postmarketing safety databases by the Applicant for both CORECTIM and ANZUPGO for lipid abnormalities (MedDRA terms “Cholesterol analyses, Triglyceride analyses, Elevated triglycerides, Elevated cholesterol, Elevated cholesterol with elevated triglycerides, Hyperlipidemia, Lipoprotein and lipid tests”) did not identify any AEs related to the lipid profile for either CORECTIM or ANZUPGO.
L560: ## Tuberculosis
L561: Search of the postmarketing safety databases by the Applicant for both CORECTIM and ANZUPGO using the MedDRA high level term “Tuberculous infections” identified no AEs of tuberculosis in either database.
L562: ## Hypoglycemia
L563: Preclinical evidence in literature reports suggest that JAK and TYK2 inhibitors may cause hypoglycemia in diabetics by protecting pancreatic \beta-cells from immune-mediated destruction and improving insulin sensitivity, thereby enhancing endogenous insulin production and action (Gurzov et al. 2016; Bako et al. 2019). European Medicines Agency labeling for oral JAK inhibitors (e.g., tofacitinib, baricitinib and upadacitinib) states that there have been reports of hypoglycemia following initiation of JAK inhibitors, and dose adjustment of antidiabetic medicinal products may be necessary in the event that hypoglycemia occurs.
L564: In order to further characterize any potential impact of delgocitinib on hypoglycemia in subjects with diabetes, the Applicant provided a cumulative review of all United States and foreign cases of hypoglycemia or blood glucose decrease associated with the use of delgocitinib cream, 2%. The postmarketing data sources for CORECTIM (ointment) and ANZUPGO (cream) included both the safety database and the medical literature (MedDRA search terms: Hypoglycemia standardized MedDRA query [SMQ] Narrow search, Blood glucose abnormal [PT] and Blood glucose fluctuation [PT]) (cutoff date of April 30, 2025). The search identified no relevant cases from the literature. However, in their safety database, the Applicant identified one case of hypoglycemia from Trial DELTA 2 in a subject who received vehicle and none in subjects treated with delgocitinib.
L565: A 19-year-old female subject with no known history of diabetes developed hypoglycemia on Day 57 of administration of vehicle. The blood glucose level was 2.3 mmol/L (41 mg/dL). No concomitant medication was administered. The subject recovered without discontinuation of vehicle. The event was reported as moderate in severity and assessed by the investigator as not related to investigational drug. On reassessment, the blood glucose level was 4.4 mmol/L (79 mg/dL).
L566: Search of the safety database for long-term safety Trial DELTA 3 (not pooled with the controlled trials) using the same MedDRA terms, confirmed that there were no events of hypoglycemia.
L567: In conclusion, the relevance of the postmarketing safety data for delgocitinib ointment, 0.5% and cream, 2% is limited due insufficient information regarding the number of patients who received treatment with delgocitinib and duration of treatment. The causality of delgocitinib in the onset of EH and HZ cannot be established. However, a role for delgocitinib cannot be excluded. The
L568: effect of delgocitinib on bone marrow cannot be assessed because routine blood work was not obtained.
L569: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L570: The Applicant submitted safety data from seven completed clinical trials in the development program for delgocitinib cream in subjects with CHE.
L571: - Two 16-week vehicle-controlled phase 3 trials (LP0133-1401/DELTA 1 and LP0133-1402/DELTA 2) in subjects with moderate to severe CHE
- A 36-week open-label single-arm extension trial (LP0133-1403/DELTA 3) in subjects with moderate to severe CHE continuing from Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
- A 24-week active comparator, open-label trial comparing delgocitinib with alitretinoin capsules (LP0133-1528 DELTA FORCE) in subjects with severe CHE
- A phase 1 PK trial (LP0133-2285)
- A phase 2 dose-ranging trial (LP0133-1273) in subjects with mild to severe CHE
- A phase 2 proof of concept trial (LP0133-1180) in subjects with at least mild CHE
L572: The Applicant submitted additional safety data from two completed clinical trials that evaluated delgocitinib cream in subjects with atopic dermatitis (phase 1 PK/MUST trial LP0133-1181 and phase 2 dose-ranging trial LP0133-1275) and three completed clinical trials that evaluated delgocitinib cream in healthy subjects (phototoxicity trial LP0133-1408, photoallergenicity trial LP0133-1411, and QT prolongation trial LP0133-1408).
L573: The review team focused the evaluation of the safety and efficacy of delgocitinib cream on data from the two vehicle-controlled, phase 3 trials (DELTA 1 and DELTA 2) and open-label extension trial DELTA 3. Analysis of the safety data related to the additional trials did not reveal any unique safety signals from those observed in trials DELTA 1, DELTA 2, and DELTA 3, and was, therefore, considered supportive (refer to Section 7.6.2 for analysis of phase 2b trials 1273 and Section 7.6.3.1 for analysis for active comparator trial DELTA FORCE).
L574: The review team identified no major data quality or integrity issues that would preclude performing a safety review for this marketing application. There were no major issues identified with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. Review of the Applicant's translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA PTs for the adverse events reported in the trials were found to be acceptable.
L575: The protocol defined treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) as "any untoward medical occurrence in a patient or clinical investigation subject administered a pharmaceutical product and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment. An AE can therefore be any unfavorable and unintended sign (including an abnormal laboratory finding), symptom, or disease temporally associated with the use of a medicinal (investigational) product, whether or not related to the medicinal (investigational) product." AEs reported in trials DELTA 1, DELTA 2, and DELTA 3 were coded according to the MedDRA (v. 24.0).
L576: Adverse event severity was described as mild, moderate, or severe according to the investigator's clinical judgement. Mild was defined as "[a]n AE that is usually transient and may require only
L577: minimal treatment or therapeutic intervention. The event does not generally interfere with usual activities of daily living,” moderate was defined as “[a]n AE that is usually alleviated with additional specific therapeutic intervention. The event interferes with usual activities of daily living, causing discomfort, but poses no significant or permanent risk of harm to the subject,” and severe was defined as “[a]n AE that interrupts usual activities of daily living, or significantly affects clinical status, or may require intensive therapeutic intervention.” Causality was assessed by the investigator as probably related, possibly related, or not related. Regardless of how causality was assessed by the investigator, the review team also reviewed case report forms and narrative summaries of subjects with adverse events that may have represented safety signals.
L578: A SAE was defined as “any untoward medical occurrence that: results in death, is life-threatening – at risk of death at the time of the SAE (not an event that hypothetically might have caused death if more severe), requires in-patient hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization, results in persistent or significant disability or incapacity, is a congenital anomaly or birth defect, is a medically important condition.”
L579: An AESI was defined as a “(serious or non-serious) event type of scientific and medical concern specific to the product or development program, for which additional monitoring may be appropriate.” The following were predefined as AESIs in trials DELTA 1, DELTA 2, and DELTA 3: eczema herpeticum, deep vein thrombosis, and pulmonary embolism. Other safety areas of interest for delgocitinib included known JAK inhibitor drug class adverse drug reactions and safety issues, the adverse drug reactions from the delgocitinib ointment CORECTIM program, and rare events.
L580: Laboratory samples in trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, were collected according to visit schedules during the trials at Weeks 0, 2, 4, 8, 12, and 16. In trial DELTA 3, laboratory samples were collected every 8 weeks. Clinically relevant laboratory findings were identified by the Applicant based on common terminology criteria for adverse events (CTCAE), Council for International Organizations of Medical Sciences, and National Cholesterol Education Program Adult Treatment Panel III guideline thresholds, and by the review team based on the FDA’s Standard Safety Tables and Figures Integrated Guide.
L581: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L582: The primary analysis dataset for the review of safety for delgocitinib cream, 2% for the treatment of moderate to severe CHE included pooled data from the vehicle-controlled phase 3 trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 from Weeks 0 to 16. The safety population includes all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of the study medication during the double-blind, 16-week treatment period in these two trials. Safety data from this population were analyzed according to the treatment that subjects actually received. In the pooled phase 3 trials, 638 subjects received delgocitinib. The safety population by trial and duration of treatment is presented below.

L596: The total subject exposure to delgocitinib cream for the treatment of moderate to severe CHE provides adequate data for the evaluation of safety. The total exposures for 16 weeks and 52 weeks are sufficient to characterize the safety of the product over short and longer treatment periods. Because of the size of the safety database, rare adverse events associated with JAKi may not have been identified. However, the potential for these adverse reactions will be communicated in labeling to inform prescribers and patients of the risks.
L597: ## **Relevant Characteristics of the Safety Population**
L598: The demographic characteristics were well balanced across treatment groups in the safety population. There were no clinically relevant differences in the demographics or baseline characteristics of subjects who received delgocitinib cream, 2% compared with vehicle. Across treatment groups in Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, the mean age of enrolled subjects was 44.1 years. Approximately 8% of subjects were 65 years of age or older, 64% were female, 90% were White, 4% were Asian, and 1% were Black. Approximately 94% of subjects identified as not Hispanic or Latino and 3% of subjects identified as Hispanic or Latino.
L599: Subtypes of CHE according to the primary etiology were atopic hand eczema (35.9%), hyperkeratotic eczema (21.5%), irritant contact dermatitis (19.6%), allergic contact dermatitis (13.9%), vesicular hand eczema (9.1%), and contact urticaria/protein contact dermatitis (0.1%). Across all trial arms, 28% of subjects were diagnosed with two or more overlapping CHE subtypes.
L600: Of all subjects, 46% were diagnosed with AD, and 1.14% were treated for AD within 28 days prior to baseline (1% with topical treatment, and 0.2% with systemic). In addition, 38.7% of subjects had an atopic diathesis (allergic rhinitis, asthma, etc.). Occupation (e.g., food handler, veterinarian, farmer, hairdresser, healthcare worker, etc.) was contributory to the development and/or chronicity of CHE in 36.6% of subjects (specific occupation was not recorded); CHE impacted employment in 60.8% of subjects. Of the 78.6% of subjects who were patch tested, 36% had positive results, and 18% of these results were considered related to CHE. A total of 29.8% of subjects were diagnosed with irritant dermatitis, and 20.2% of these were diagnosed
L601: with contact allergic dermatitis. All subjects had received prior systemic therapy, which included oral corticosteroids. A total of 12% of subjects previously received alitretinoin, 7.4% of subjects received traditional immunosuppressive agents, 0.6% of subjects received dupilumab, 0.2% of subjects received other biologics, and 4.7% other therapies.
L602: The demographics and baseline characteristics of the long-term safety pool (52 weeks) were consistent with the short-term safety pool (16 weeks), as expected, since the same subjects continued from Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 into Trial DELTA 3.
L603: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L604: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L605: The overall incidence, severity, and actions taken in response to the TEAEs were generally well balanced between the delgocitinib and vehicle groups. However, there were more AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of the study drug for subjects in the vehicle group. All AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of the study product in the delgocitinib cream group were single events with no clustering of events at the PT level. Table 23 below provides an overview of the TEAEs reported during the 16-week, vehicle-controlled phase 3 Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2.

L616: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Trials DELTA 1, DELTA 2 and DELTA 3
L617: There were three deaths in the development program:
L618: - A 63-year-old male subject ( [REDACTED] (b)(6) ) with a medical history of CHE, obesity, hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and prior tobacco use, died due to metastatic esophageal cancer. The subject received vehicle cream for 16 weeks during Trial DELTA 2 and subsequently received a single application of delgocitinib cream, 2% in extension Trial DELTA 3 before discontinuing treatment.
L619: The trial investigator assessed the death as unrelated to the study product and this reviewer agrees. The subject had multiple risk factors for esophageal cancer, including obesity, history of smoking, older age, and male sex, with minimal exposure to delgocitinib (a single application). The nature and timeline of disease progression are more consistent with a preexisting malignancy than with any plausible drug-induced effect.
L620: - A 72-year-old male subject ( [REDACTED] (b)(6) ) with a complex medical history, including CHE, hypertension, epilepsy, renal cell carcinoma, chronic alcohol use, hepatitis B virus infection, and liver cirrhosis, died of unknown causes on Day 362. At the time of death, he had been on study drug continuously for approximately 4 months and intermittently for approximately 8 months
L621: The subject initially presented to the emergency department with a left-sided peripheral facial nerve palsy. Evaluation included normal head computed tomography (CT) and ECG. Laboratory testing revealed elevated inflammatory markers (C-reactive protein, gamma-glutamyl transferase, aspartate aminotransferase [AST]), thrombocytopenia (platelet count 50 \times 10^3/\mu\text{L}), and negative COVID-19 status. He was discharged the same day with prednisolone and pantoprazole. Subsequent diagnostic imaging and biopsy results were not available. The subject fell within 2 weeks of discharge, was rehospitalized, and died approximately 1 week later.
L622: The trial investigator assessed the death as unrelated to the study product and provided no alternative etiology. This reviewer agrees; given the subject's advanced age, multiple comorbidities (including cirrhosis and a history of malignancy), and recent fall, the death is unlikely to be attributable to delgocitinib. However, due to the limited clinical information a contributory role for delgocitinib cannot be definitively ruled out.
L623: - A 72-year-old male subject ( [REDACTED] (b)(6) ) with a medical history of CHE and hypertension experienced a fatal myocardial infarction (MI) approximately 218 days (about 7 months) after his last dose of delgocitinib cream, 2%. The subject had completed 16 weeks of treatment in Trial DELTA 2 and an additional 4 weeks of treatment in Trial DELTA 3.
L624: The subject had been receiving antihypertensive therapy for 3 months prior to the event. He died 10 days after the MI. The trial investigator assessed the death as unrelated to the study drug and attributed it to the myocardial infarction. This reviewer concurs with that
L625: assessment, given the extended time interval (&gt;31 weeks) between the last exposure to delgocitinib and the onset of the SAE.
L626: However, due to the limited information available regarding the subject's other potential cardiovascular risk factors, as well as the known association between JAKi and MACE, a contributory role of delgocitinib cannot be definitively ruled out.
L627: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L628: During the initial treatment period for trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, SAEs were reported at a comparable rate in the delgocitinib group and the vehicle group (Table 24 below). SAEs occurred at a rate of &lt;1% for all system organ classes (SOCs) in the delgocitinib and vehicle groups. The highest risk difference in the pooled trials occurred with the SOC of infections and infestations (0.3%). For this SOC, four subjects reported four events in the delgocitinib cream group (COVID-19 pneumonia, keratitis bacterial, peritonsillar abscess, and tonsillitis) and no subjects reported events in the vehicle group. None of the events were considered related to treatment by the investigator nor clinical reviewer, and there was no clustering of events over time. For all other SOCs, the risk difference was 0.2%. Within all SOCs, SAEs were reported as single events within each treatment group, with no clustering at the PT level.
L629: One subject in the delgocitinib group experienced the SAE hand dermatitis which required hospitalization. The narrative summary is below:
L630: - A 31-year-old female subject (b) (6) enrolled in Trial DELTA 2, with history of hospitalization related to CHE and AD (inactive at baseline), was hospitalized for an exacerbation of CHE on Day 115. The subject received delgocitinib twice daily until Day 85 when she discontinued treatment due to lack of efficacy. She noted an increase in the severity of CHE on Day 70 and was hospitalized on Day 115 for 11 days “due to worsening of hand eczema.” Her treatment included “bath-PUVA, clobetasol ointment, triclosan cream with betamethasone, liquor carbonis detergens 5% ointment, and oral antihistamines.” The reason for the prolonged hospitalization was not stated. The investigator and Applicant both assessed the SAE as unrelated to the IMP. However, given limited information, the clinical reviewer cannot rule out a causal association with delgocitinib.

L642: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Pooled Analyses, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2
L643: During the 16-week vehicle-controlled period, AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of study drug were few and more frequent in the vehicle group than in the delgocitinib group (refer to Table 25 and Table 26 below). In subjects who received delgocitinib, no PTs were reported in &gt;1 subject. The following AEs leading to discontinuation were reported in subjects who received delgocitinib and none in subjects who received vehicle: skin bacterial infection, contact dermatitis, and eczema.
L644: Narrative summaries of the subjects who discontinued delgocitinib due to skin bacterial infection and contact dermatitis are below:
L645: - Skin bacterial infection: A 38-year-old female ( (b) (6) ) with a history of allergic rhinitis developed a nonserious, moderate severity “skin bacterial infection” of the hand on the first day of treatment with delgocitinib cream. No further details are provided. The AE is reported resolved on Day 13 and the investigator assessed the AE as unrelated to the IMP. Given limited information, this reviewer cannot rule out a causal association.
- Contact dermatitis: A 38-year-old female ( (b) (6) ) with a history of CHE developed “contact dermatitis” of the hands on Day 9 of treatment with delgocitinib cream. The subject reported erythema, edema, and papules, which resolved 3 days after discontinuation of IMP. An IMP patch test was negative, which suggests that this event was not due to allergy but rather irritant contact dermatitis. The event was considered possibly related to IMP by investigator and clinical reviewer.
