Investigational medicinal product	Name of IMP: delgocitinib cream.
Active substance: delgocitinib.
Dosage form: cream.
Concentration: 20 mg/g and cream vehicle.
Dose and method of administration: topical application twice daily.

Duration of trial participation	The duration of trial participation will be up to 22 weeks, consisting of a screening period of up to 4 weeks, a treatment period of 16 weeks, and a safety follow-up period of 2 weeks (safety follow-up period not applicable for subjects participating in the LTE trial).
Number of subjects	A total of 450 eligible subjects will be randomised in a 2:1 ratio to delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g or cream vehicle.
Number and distribution of trial sites	Approximately 55 sites in Europe and Canada.
Statistical methods	
For the primary and key secondary endpoints, confirmatory one-sided (superiority) hypotheses will be tested for delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g versus cream vehicle based on the primary analysis for the primary estimand. A closed testing procedure with hierarchical tests, alpha splitting, and alpha recycling will be used to control the overall type I error at a nominal one-sided 2.5% level. The statistical testing strategy is built on the principle that the IGA-CHE TS superiority at Week 16 will have to be established before testing for additional benefits (key secondary endpoints) related to efficacy and health-related quality of life.
The primary estimand for the primary and key secondary endpoints will use a 'composite' strategy where the occurrence of pre-defined IEs (initiation of rescue treatment and permanent discontinuation of IMP) is a component of the endpoint. A first supplementary estimand will use a 'pandemic modified composite' strategy which follows a 'composite' strategy for IEs independent of the COVID-19 pandemic, attempting to quantify the effect of the randomised treatment in a world without COVID-19 pandemic. A second supplementary estimand will use a 'treatment policy' strategy which attempts to quantify the effect of the randomised treatment regardless of whether an IE occurs. The second supplementary estimand will be used for endpoints related to Week 16 (primary endpoint visit).
The primary analysis for the primary estimand for the binary endpoints will be CMH test stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE score. The primary analysis for the primary estimand for the continuous endpoints will be ANCOVA with effects of treatment group, region, baseline IGA-CHE score, and baseline value (endpoint of interest).

Title of trial	A phase 3 extension trial of DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 to evaluate the long-term safety of a twice-daily treatment with delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g as needed for up to 36 weeks in adult subjects with chronic hand eczema (DELTA 3)
Short title of trial	An open-label multisite extension trial in subjects who completed the LP0133-1401 (DELTA 1) or LP0133-1402 (DELTA 2) trials
Main objectives
and endpoints	Objectives
Primary objective:
To evaluate the long-term safety of an as-needed treatment with twice-daily applications of delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g.
Secondary objective:
To evaluate the long-term efficacy of an as-needed treatment with twice-daily applications of delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g.	Endpoints
Primary endpoints:Number of treatment-emergent AEs from baseline up to Week 38.
Secondary endpoints:IGA-CHE1 score at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36.

HECSI2 score at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36.
HECSI-75 at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36.
HECSI-90 at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36.

The IGA-CHE is an instrument used in clinical trials to rate the severity of the subject's global chronic hand eczema (CHE) and is based on a 5-point scale ranging from 0 (clear) to 4 (severe). The HECSI is an instrument used in clinical trials to rate the severity of 6 clinical signs (erythema, infiltration/papulation, vesicles, fissures, scaling, and oedema) and the extent of the lesions on each of the 5 hand areas (fingertips, fingers [except fingertips], palm of hands, back of hands, and wrists) by use of standard scales.
Abbreviations: AE = adverse event; HECSI = Hand Eczema Severity Index; HECSI-75 = at least 75% improvement in HECSI score from baseline in parent trial; HECSI-90 = at least 90% improvement in HECSI score from baseline in parent trial; IGA-CHE = Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema.
Final collection of
data for the
primary endpoint	Week 38
Trial design	Subjects who completed the treatment period in the parent trials (LP0133-1401 or LP0133-1402) will be offered the opportunity to participate in this extension trial.
L2048: In the parent trials, subjects were treated either with delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g or cream vehicle twice daily for 16 weeks.
The trial will include a screening period of about 4 weeks (during participation in the parent trial). The baseline visit in this extension trial will coincide with the Week 16 visit (end-of-treatment visit) in the parent trial.
From baseline to Week 36, subjects will be treated on an as-needed basis with delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g twice daily, i.e., at any time during the trial, if a subject has an IGA-CHE score \ge 2, the investigator will dispense delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g and instruct the subject to start treatment with twice-daily applications. When the subject achieves IGA-CHE score of 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) the investigator will instruct the subject to stop treatment.
Between baseline and Week 36, subjects will attend site visits every 4 weeks. If the subject experiences worsening of CHE signs and symptoms between scheduled visits, an unscheduled visit should be planned as soon as possible in order to decide if treatment with delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g should be started. Similarly, once symptoms resolve, the subject should visit the site as soon as possible (either at a planned visit or unscheduled visit) so that the investigator can assess if treatment should be stopped.
A follow-up phone visit will be conducted approximately 2 weeks after the end-of-treatment/early termination visit.

Abbreviations: IGA-CHE: Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema. W: week.

Efficacy assessments:
HECSI and IGA-CHE.

The baseline visit in this extension trial must coincide with the Week 16 (end-of-treatment) visit in the parent trial.
Subjects must have met eligibility criteria at screening and baseline in the parent trial.
Subjects must have completed the treatment period in the parent trial (to be assessed at baseline visit in this extension trial).
Subjects must have complied with the clinical trial protocol in the parent trial to the satisfaction of the investigator.
A woman of childbearing potential must use an acceptable method of birth control throughout the trial up until the end-of-treatment/early termination visit.

Statistical methods	
Primary endpoint:
Treatment-emergent AEs (AEs emerging from baseline in this extension trial up to Week 38) will be summarised for all enrolled subjects and will be presented by Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred term and primary system organ class (SOC). AEs will be summarised in terms of the number and percentage of subjects with AEs, the number of AEs, and the rate of AEs (number of AEs per 100 person years of observation time).
Secondary endpoints:
IGA-CHE and HECSI scores will be summarised at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36.
The proportion of subjects with IGA-CHE score of 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) will be summarised at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36.
HECSI-75 and HECSI-90 will be summarised at each scheduled visit from baseline up to Week 36. For the calculation of HECSI-75 and HECSI-90, the baseline score from the parent trial will be used.
If nothing else is stated, an observed-cases approach will be used for tabulations of data by visit (that is, involving only those subjects who attended each specific visit).
For binary response tabulations, subjects experiencing discontinuation of IMP, initiation of rescue treatment, or withdrawal from trial, will be imputed as nonresponders. Otherwise, missing values will not be imputed.

L2070: ## 16.1.2. COA Content Validity
L2071: ## Investigator's Global Assessment for Chronic Hand Eczema and Hand Eczema Severity Index
L2072: The Applicant did not complete instrument development activities to evaluate the content validity of the IGA-CHE or the HECSI. The clinician interviews conducted provided some information related to the content of the instruments.
L2073: ## Hand Eczema Symptom Diary and Hand Eczema Impact Scale
L2074: The Applicant completed the following instrument development activities to evaluate the content validity of the HESD and HEIS:
L2075: - Literature review
- Expert input (concept elicitation interviews)
- Patient input (concept elicitation, cognitive interviews)
L2076: A high-level summary of the results for each activity is described below. Refer to the COA evidence dossier for full details regarding the methodology and results of each activity.
L2077: ## 16.1.2.1. Expert Input
L2078: Transcripts of interviews with clinical experts were provided for review, along with a qualitative report analyzing the data. The Applicant conducted 60-minute qualitative concept-elicitation interviews with dermatologists (n=5) via telephone to understand the signs/symptoms and impacts of CHE from a clinical perspective, particularly the relative clinical importance of each sign/symptom and associated impact on the patient.
L2079: Dermatologists from the United States (n=2), Germany (n=2), and Denmark (n=1) were interviewed. Most of the clinicians were female (n=3/5, 60%) with a mean age of 57.8 years (range: 52 to 65 years). Four were academic professors/dermatologists (80%), while the other was a specialist in dermatological immunology (20%). The mean length of time in position was 21.6 years (range 9 to 35) and the mean length of time treating patients with hand eczema was 26 years (range: 15 to 35 years). The mean number of hand eczema patients seen in the last year was 230 (range: 50 to 500). The most frequently spontaneously reported signs by all clinicians (n=5/5, 100%) were itch, redness, cracking, dryness, and pain. Experts noted that signs/symptoms associated with encountering allergens or irritants and representing an inflammation of the skin on the hands occurred at the same time as CHE signs/symptoms, although this does not necessarily indicate that the symptoms are the same or that they had a direct impact or cause on the other (i.e., they may present simultaneously). For example, redness, pain, and swelling (all associated with inflammation) were most commonly reported to occur at the same time.
L2080: ## 16.1.2.2. Patient Input
L2081: ## 16.1.2.2.1. Concept Elicitation Interviews
L2082: The Applicant conducted two rounds of 60-minute qualitative, face-to-face, concept elicitation interviews with 20 patients (N=20) with CHE in the United States to 1) explore the signs/symptoms and impacts of CHE from the patient perspective and 2) use the findings to update the conceptual model to include any aspects of the patient experience of CHE not included based on the literature review.
L2083: A total of 30 distinct signs/symptoms associated with CHE were reported. Of those signs/symptoms, “itch” (n=20/20, 100%), “redness” (n=20/20, 100%), and “cracking” (n=20/20, 100%), were reported by all subjects and nearly all subjects (n=19/20, 95%), regardless of CHE subtype, reported that cracked skin was a relevant symptom of their CHE. Nearly all subjects reported thickening of the skin (n=17/20, 85%) and pain in their hands (n=18/20, 90%). Concept saturation across all five patient groups was demonstrated for the following in descending order (overall n, %) based on patients who spontaneously reported the concept: itch (n=19/20, 95%), redness/erythema (n=17/20, 85%), dryness (n=17/20, 85%), burning (n=13/20, 65%), roughened texture (n=12/20, 60%), cracking/fissuring (n=11/20, 55%), flaking (n=10/20, 50%), bleeding (n=9/20, 45%), and raw/broken skin (n=9/20, 45%). Patients reported that CHE signs/symptoms affect eight domains: sleep (n=16/20, 80%), physical functioning (n=12/20, 60%), daily activities (n=15/20, 75%), emotional well-being (n=16/20, 80%), social functioning (n=7/20, 35%), work (n=12/20, 60%), finances (n=6/20, 30%), and use of topical treatments to alleviate symptoms of CHE (20/20, 100%). They reported the following proximal daily limitations during the interview: avoiding certain soaps and chemicals (n=15/20, 75%); difficulty completing household chores, particularly those that involve getting hands wet (n=13/20, 65%); difficulty performing self-care activities (n=12/20, 60%); and an impact on leisure activities (n=8/20, 40%).
L2084: Of note, nearly all (n=19/20, 95%) patients reported at least one environmental factor that they perceived either triggered or exacerbated their CHE symptoms, including various allergens and/or chemicals (n=10/20, 50%), cold temperatures (particularly the winter) (n=9/20, 45%), excessive hand washing (n=3/20, 15%), warm temperatures (n=4/20, 20%), stress (n=2/20, 10%), sun exposure (n=1/20, 5%), and humidity (n=1/20, 5%). Fifteen patients (n=15/20, 75%) reported avoiding touching detergents, soaps, and chemicals or stated they always wear gloves when using detergents and cleaning products.
L2085: Based on the qualitative research, the concepts of itch, pain, cracking, redness, dryness, and flaking included in the HESD appear relevant to the target population. Patients were not asked which were the most important or most bothersome signs/symptoms.
L2086: The results of the literature review and the qualitative concept elicitation interviews informed the item generation and development of the HESD. Of the 30 distinct signs/symptoms that were reported in the patient concept elicitation interviews and reported by expert dermatologists, 13 concepts were drafted into PRO items. The remaining 17 signs/symptoms were excluded based on infrequent reports from patients during the interviews and least clinical relevance as reported by the expert dermatologists.
L2087: ## 16.1.2.2.2. Cognitive Interviews
L2088: The Applicant conducted two rounds of 60-minute, face-to-face, cognitive interviews with 20 patients in the United States (n=10 per round) with a clinician-confirmed diagnosis of CHE to evaluate the content relevance and comprehension of the HESD. A total of 15 items were debriefed across the interviews. In all, 13 items were debriefed in the first round of interviews, and the same 13 items, plus an additional 2 items were tested in the second round of interviews.
L2089: HESD items were well understood (e.g., N=20/20, 100%, understood items assessing dryness, roughness, flaking, bleeding, swelling, redness, cracking, pain, and burning) and all participants reported that items assessing itch, redness, roughness, and dryness were relevant, regardless of CHE subtype. Items assessing swelling, bleeding, heat, thickening, and blisters were the least relevant, but all were still experienced by 14 or more participants (&gt;70%). Five participants (n=5/20, 25%) appeared to have difficulty understanding the item or recall period or did not believe the recall period was relevant for a given item. The greatest conceptual overlap was reported between flaking and cracking (n=6/7), heat and burning (n=5/7), hardness and rough skin (n=5/7), and hardness and thickened skin (n=5/7). Due to the conceptual overlap between items pertaining to hardness, thickness, and roughness of the skin, and because these signs were relatively less relevant to patients, hardness and roughness were removed. The item referring to blisters was also removed from the HESD.
L2090: HEIS items were understood by participants, although a few had difficulty understanding questions related to impacts to work (n=3), the use of soaps/cleaning products (n=1) and disliking the appearance of the hands (n=1). In terms of relevance, most patients found the questions to be pertinent (especially dislike of appearance, embarrassment, self-consciousness, and soap/cleaning products). However, touching objects, wearing gloves, and work were less relevant, based on CHE type and physician-rated CHE severity. In terms of relevance, items assessing feeling annoyed, frustration, and disliking the appearance of the hands were the most relevant items for patients irrespective of CHE subtype. Items assessing the impact to patient's ability to hold objects (n=9/19, 47%), touch objects (n=8/19, 42%), and get dressed (n=9/19, 47%) were reportedly the least relevant.
L2091: ## 16.1.3. COA Scoring Algorithms
L2092: ## 16.1.3.1. Investigator's Global Assessment for Chronic Hand Eczema
L2093: The IGA-CHE generates a single item score that ranges from 0 to 4, with higher scores indicating greater CHE severity.
L2094: ## 16.1.3.2. Hand Eczema Symptom Diary
L2095: The HESD generates single item scores and a total score. The single item scores range from 0 to 10, with higher scores indicating greater symptom severity. The total score ranges from 0 to 80 with higher scores indicating a greater symptom severity.
L2096: The weekly HESD score was the average of the seven evening diary entries prior to each site visit by the patient. A weekly average was calculated as long as four or more of the seven diary entries were completed. The averages were calculated for the HESD total score.
L2097: ## 16.1.3.3. Hand Eczema Severity Index
L2098: The HECSI generates a total score, ranging from 0 to 360, with higher scores indicating a greater severity of hand eczema.
L2099: The total score is calculated as follows:
L2100: - Clinical Signs: For each of the given five areas of the hand (i.e., fingertips, fingers, palms, back of hands and wrists), the severity of each sign is scored on a 0 to 3 scale (0=none, 1 slight, 2=moderate, 3=severe).
- Extent of Involvement: The extent of lesions in each area is assessed based on the percentage of surface area involved and given a value between 0 and 4 (0=0%, 1=1 to 25%, 2=26 to 50%, 3=51 to 75%, 4=76 to 100%).
- The total HECSI score is calculated by multiplying the extent score for each location by the total sum of the intensity of each clinical feature, and then summing these scores across all locations.
L2101: There was insufficient evidence supporting the validity and reliability of the scoring for the HECSI.
L2102: ## 16.1.3.4. Hand Eczema Impact Scale
L2103: The HEIS PDAL generates a domain score that ranges from 0 to 4, with higher scores indicating a higher impact. The HEIS PDAL domain score is the average of the three items in that domain. Scores are calculated when \ge 50\% of the items are present.
L2104: Given that multiple functional impacts for CHE co-occur with other dermatologic disease, the HEIS PDAL was not considered specific to CHE.
L2105: ## 16.1.4. IGA-CHE Test-Retest Reliability
L2106: Assessing inter-rater and intrarater reliability of ClinROs helps ensure that scores are consistent within a given rater as well as across different raters. While the Applicant did not collect data to assess inter- or intrarater reliability of the IGA-CHE, the Applicant evaluated the test-retest reliability of IGA-CHE scores. Test-retest reliability measures the degree to which scores are similar at different time points in a subset of “stable” subjects. Using data from Trial DELTA 1, the Applicant calculated Cohen’s weighted Kappa with quadratic weights1 across two time points: Week 2 to Week 4 and Week 4 to Week 8, using the PaGA, HESD PGI-S, and HECSI to
L2107: 1 The use of quadratic weights penalize larger differences more heavily and is generally preferred for ordinal scales such as the IGA-CHE.
L2108: define stable patients.2 The Kappa coefficients reported in Table 130 indicate adequate test-retest reliability for IGA-CHE scores at both timepoints, using all three COAs to define stable subjects.
L2109: **Table 130. Test-Retest Reliability: IGA-CHE Score Weighted Kappa Coefficient Estimates and 95% Confidence Interval Between Week 2 and Week 4 and Between Week 4 and Week 8, Trial DELTA 1**
L2110: Time Point	Stability Definition	N (%)	Kappa Coefficient	95% Confidence Interval
Lower	Upper
Week 2 to Week 4	PaGA	142 (50.7%)	0.625	0.525	0.725
HESD PGI-S	154 (55.0%)	0.678	0.592	0.764
HECSI	218 (77.9%)	0.690	0.615	0.766
Week 4 to Week 8	PaGA	144 (51.4%)	0.761	0.687	0.834
HESD PGI-S	161 (57.5%)	0.760	0.692	0.827
HECSI	227 (81.1%)	0.756	0.699	0.812
L2111: Source: Table 10 in LC7839J ICA-CHE report v1.0.
L2112: Note: Stable patients were defined as follows: The PaGA population consists of subjects who reported a change in PaGA=0 between the two time points. The HESD PGI-S population consists of subjects who reported a change in HESD PGI-S=0 between the two time points. The HECSI population consists of subjects who have HECSI change score &lt;0.50 baseline SD between the two time points.
L2113: Abbreviations: HECSI: Hand Eczema Severity Index; HESD PGI-S, Chronic Hand Eczema Symptom Diary Patient Global Impression of Severity; N, sample size.; PaGA, Patient Global Assessment of Disease Severity
L2114: While the Kappa coefficient point estimates appear adequate, the lower bound of the 95% confidence intervals indicate lower reliability, especially between Week 2 and Week 4. Notably, these reliability estimates were calculated using data collected from subjects on treatment. As the test-retest reliability of scores should be assessed in the absence of any intervening effects other than time, the Agency sent the Applicant an information request on February 13, 2025, requesting additional test-retest reliability analyses looking at (1) all subjects (pooled across treatment arms) at Screening (i.e., Visit 1) and Baseline (i.e., Visit 2), and (2) vehicle subjects only at Baseline and Week 2 in each trial separately. The Applicant provided a response on February 27, 2025 (see Table 131). The results indicate moderate test-retest reliability between Screening and Baseline and poor test-retest reliability based on vehicle subjects only between Baseline and Week 2. The Applicant states lower Kappa coefficient estimates between Screening and Baseline as compared to between Week 2 and Week 4 and between Week 4 and Week 8 may be in part due to “the wash out period between screening and baseline, reflecting the dynamic and fluctuating nature of the disease coupled with effects of treatment withdrawal.” The Applicant states the poor test-retest reliability between Baseline and Week 2 for vehicle subjects may be due to “a known placebo effect in addition to the cream vehicle effect, in the first few weeks following randomization especially, leading to a lack of stability for control subjects.” The Applicant’s claims appear reasonable.

The HECSI population consists of subjects who have HECSI change score &lt;0.50 baseline SD between the two time points.

L2120: Taken together, these results suggest IGA-CHE scores may be sufficiently stable over time and consistently reflect subject's CHE symptoms.
L2121: Results showed a graduate decline in HESD total scores from Baseline (mean =7.0, SD =1.50) to Week 16 (mean =3.6, SD =2.84).
L2122: While there were high levels of missing data (74 datapoints) at Week 16, it is unlikely that any missing data with contrary results (i.e., no change or an increase in symptoms) would have affected the pattern of decreased scores across the treatment period.
L2123: Anchor scales – Patient Global Impression of Change for itch, pain, and HESD, IGA-CHE, and PaGA – showed comparable improvements over the treatment period.

L2130: ## 16.1.5. HESD Itch Score and HESD Pain Score
L2131: Test-retest reliability evaluation of the HESD Itch score (weekly average) and HESD Pain score (weekly average) showed acceptable reliability with intra-class correlation coefficients ranging from 0.93 to 0.98. Other validity evidence (i.e., convergent, known groups) clearly demonstrated that HESD Itch and Pain scores discriminated between groups expected to differ in symptom severity and were sensitive to change over time. Meaningful change thresholds were evaluated by the Applicant, supported by anchor-based analyses and empirical cumulative distribution function plots, with evidence supporting a 4-point threshold for improvement for HESD Itch and HESD Pain scores.
L2132: ## 16.1.6. HECSI
L2133: The Applicant submitted psychometric analyses for the HECSI using data from their phase 2b study (Trial LP-0133-1273), which included *Change in HECSI score from Baseline to Week 16* as a secondary endpoint. As the assessment of meaningful change is specific to the endpoint being interpreted, and the HECSI-based endpoints being interpreted in Trial DELTA 1 and Trial DELTA 2 were based on a percent change from Baseline at Week 16 (see Section 6.2.3), the analyses reported in the Applicant's original submission could not be used to aid in the interpretation of the Trial DELTA 1 or Trial DELTA 2 HECSI-based endpoint scores. Despite the limitations regarding the content validity of the HECSI (see Section 6.3.2), for completeness, the Agency sent an information request to the Applicant on February 13, 2025, to request additional anchor-based analyses using the PaGA as an anchor to aid in the interpretation of percent change from Baseline at Week 16 in HECSI-based endpoint scores, using the FAS in each trial separately. The Applicant provided the requested analyses on February 27, 2025.
L2134: The correlations between change from Baseline to Week 16 in PaGA score and percent change from baseline at Week 16 in HECSI scores were moderate (see Table 133). Notably, the Pearson correlation estimates are weaker than the corresponding Spearman correlation estimates. This can likely be attributed to the range restriction in PaGA scores at baseline due to the inclusion criteria of a score of moderate or severe on the IGA-CHE, which assesses a similar concept as the PaGA. Such a range restriction would suggest the Spearman correlation coefficient is more appropriate than the Pearson correlation coefficient to estimate the relationship between the change in PaGA and HECSI from Baseline to Week 16. These Spearman correlations suggest the PaGA may have utility in deriving the amount of change in HECSI-based endpoint scores that represents a meaningful benefit to patients.
L2135: **Table 133. Correlations Between Change From Baseline to Week 16 in PaGA Score and Percent Change From Baseline at Week 16 in HECSI Score, Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 (FAS)**
L2136: Trial	Pearson Correlation	Spearman Correlation
DELTA 1	0.471	0.634
DELTA 2	0.434	0.606

L2139: As this analysis was not part of the Applicant's original submission, there was no prespecified target anchor change category to indicate the amount of change on the PaGA that was
L2140: meaningful to subjects. Table 134 and Table 135 show cross-tabulations of PaGA responses at Baseline and Week 16 in both trials. Most subjects reported moderate or severe symptoms at baseline for Trial DELTA 1 and Trial DELTA 2, respectively. Across both trials, most subjects experienced either a 1-category improvement or no change from Baseline to Week 16 with the exception of subjects who reported severe symptoms at Baseline and were most likely to experience a 2-category improvement or 1-category improvement.

L2149: Given these observations in PaGA, the review team determined that it would be reasonable for the anchor-based analyses to focus on a 1-category improvement as the primary target anchor-change.
L2150: Figure 33 and Figure 34 show the empirical cumulative distribution function curves for the percent change in HECI from Baseline to Week 16 by change in PaGA from Baseline to Week 16 for Trial DELTA 1 and Trial DELTA 2, respectively. In Trial DELTA 1, the median percent change in HECI score for subjects who experienced a 1-category improvement in PaGA is 58%. However, at this value, more than 20% of subjects who reported no change or worsening on the PaGA would be misclassified as having improved on the HECI. A percent change of at least 77% would be needed to minimize the number of subjects who would be misclassified as having meaningfully improved on the HECI-based endpoint scores. In Trial DELTA 2, the median percent change in HECI score for subjects who experienced a 1-category improvement in PaGA is 69%. However, at this value, more than 20% of subjects who reported no change or worsening on the PaGA would be misclassified as having improved on the HECI. A percent change of at least 82% would be needed to minimize the number of subjects who would be misclassified as having meaningfully improved on the HECI-based endpoint scores.

In Trial DELTA 1, a percent change of at least 72.5% would be needed so that 50% of subjects who reported moderate symptoms on the PaGA at Baseline would be classified as having experienced a meaningful improvement (see Table 136). For Trial DELTA 2, a percent change of at least 69% would be needed so that 50% of subjects who reported moderate symptoms on the PaGA at baseline would be classified as having experienced a meaningful improvement (see Table 137).

L2166: While HECSI scores themselves have limited interpretability due to a lack of evidence to support the content validity of the scores (see Section 6.3.2), for completeness, the Agency requested the Applicant conduct anchor-based analyses using percent change in HECSI scores to aid in the interpretation of the multiplicity-controlled HECSI-based endpoints in Trial DELTA 1 and Trial DELTA 2. Based on the anchor-based analyses conducted using the PaGA as an anchor measure, taking into account the full distribution of HECSI percent change scores, subjects' baseline symptom severity, and the misclassification of subjects who reported no improvement or worsening on the PaGA, the Agency determined a plausible meaningful change range for percent change in HECSI scores from Baseline to Week 16 of between a 69- and 82-percentage point improvement. However, these meaningful change analyses have limited interpretability as it is unclear what the HECSI score represents (i.e., did not clinically characterize the severity and extent of hand eczema) and therefore what change (or percent change) in HECSI scores means.
L2167: ## 16.1.7. Hand Eczema Impact Scale
L2168: Meaningful within-person change scores were not evaluated for the HEIS because it did not meet the requisite criteria for content validity.
L2169: ## 16.2. Different Approaches for Handling Missing Data and Intercurrent Events
L2170: Table 138 and Table 139 present the number and percentage of subjects with missing data for the primary efficacy endpoint for Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, respectively. The tables also present the results of the sensitivity analyses for handling missing data as well as the supplementary analyses for handling the ICEs. The sensitivity analyses for the primary estimand, including the tipping point analysis, supported the conclusion of the primary analysis. The results were similar across the various imputation methods in both trials. The Applicant's tipping point analysis did not tip (i.e., p\text{-value}\ge 0.05) for any of the iterations evaluated by the Applicant. Under the extreme case (i.e., subjects with missing data in the delgocitinib group are imputed as nonresponders and subjects with missing data in the vehicle group are imputed as responders), delgocitinib remained significantly superior to vehicle (p\text{-values} &lt; 0.05) in both trials. The primary analysis of the first supplementary estimand (pandemic-modified composite) and the second supplementary estimand (treatment policy) also led to the same conclusion as the primary analysis of the primary estimand (composite).

L2193: ## 16.3. Findings in Special/Subgroup Populations
L2194: Results for the subgroup analyses by age (&lt;65 and ≥65 years), sex, race, ethnicity, region, and baseline IGA-CHE score are presented in Table 140 and Table 141 for Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2, respectively. For Trial DELTA 1, the treatment effect was higher in males and in the North America subgroup for region. However, for Trial DELTA 2, the treatment effect was generally consistent across all the subgroups for sex and region. For race and ethnicity, the sample sizes for some of the subgroups were relatively small; therefore, it would be difficult to detect any differences in efficacy between these subgroups and their complements.

L2250: ## 18. Clinical Virology
L2251: Not applicable.
L2252: ## 19. Clinical Microbiology
L2253: One phase 2 clinical trial (Trial 1273) was conducted which had the exploratory objectives of evaluating the effect of delgocitinib on *Staphylococcus aureus* colonization of the skin, skin microbiome, skin barrier function, and skin inflammation. The trial design and results are described in Section 3.2 of this review. To test the exploratory endpoints of evaluating the change in *S. aureus* colonization from Baseline to Week 16, swabs were collected from lesion and nonlesion areas at the Baseline visit and Week-16 visit. The skin swabs were used to determine *S. aureus* colonization levels using quantitative polymerase chain reaction (qPCR).
L2254: For *S. aureus* colonization, DNA using a QIAGEN robot and extraction kit was extracted from the skin swabs. The extracted DNA was used in a qPCR analysis using probes targeting the *femA* gene, a universal 16S rDNA probe, and a 18S rDNA probe. The signal from the *femA* probes was compared to a standard curve created using DNA from *S. aureus* ATCC 7000699DQ and the genome copy number was calculated. No information was provided in either trial study report or the bioanalytical report regarding quality control for the DNA extraction or qPCR method. Additionally, no data were provided to show correlation between genome copy number and colony forming units (CFUs).
L2255: Approximately half of the samples did not have detectable levels of *S. aureus* (limit of detection was reported as approximately 2 CFUs/cm2). The lack of detectable *S. aureus* results in a limited number of subjects available for analysis who had a successful clinical outcome (Table 147). A reduction in colonization levels of *S. aureus* was observed for most of the subjects receiving delgocitinib irrespective of the clinical outcome of treatment. Increases in bacterial load were seen in samples from nonlesional areas in subjects with clinical failure who received 3 mg/g of delgocitinib or vehicle control (Figure 35). Samples from either lesion or nonlesional areas of subjects with clinical success who received vehicle control showed at least a 2-fold increase in *S. aureus* burden.
L2256: Despite the difference in bacterial burden observed in subjects who received vehicle control compared to those who received higher doses (8 mg/g or 20 mg/g) of delgocitinib, it is not possible to determine the effect of delgocitinib on *S. aureus* colonization due to the lack of quality control data regarding the enumeration method, the lack of comparison between the method used (qPCR) compared to the standard colony counts, and the small population of subjects in each arm with detectable microbiological data.

L2269: ## 23. Labeling: Key Changes
L2270: This Prescribing Information (PI) review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized PI as compared to the Applicant's draft PI (Table 148). The PI was reviewed to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare practitioner.
L2271: In addition, the Applicant submitted draft [REDACTED] (b) (4). The review team revised [REDACTED] (b) (4) medication guide due to the addition of subsection 5.4 in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling (potential risk related to JAK inhibitors). Changing this document [REDACTED] (b) (4) will ensure distribution to patients per CFR 208.24 Distributing and Dispensing a Medication Guide (refer to Patient Labeling Review by Division of Medical Policy Programs and Office of Prescription Drug Promotion dated April 23, 2025).
L2272: **Table 148. Key Labeling Changes and Considerations**
L2273: Full PI Sections1	Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to Applicant's Draft PI
1 INDICATIONS AND USAGE	Limitations of Use statement was added
Language was added to the administration instructions to emphasize that ANZUPGO is to be applied "only" to the hands and wrists. This dosage- and administration-related information is particularly critical to the safe and effective use of the drug. Nonadherence to this recommendation may result in increased systemic exposure to this JAK inhibitor.
2 DOSAGE AND ADMINISTRATION	Language was added stating "Do not use more than one 30 gram tube per 2 weeks or one 60 gram tube per month." Refer to Clinical Pharmacology section of this review for rationale.

5.4 Potential Risks Related to JAK Inhibition
The Applicant proposed inclusion of application site reactions.
6 ADVERSE REACTIONS	The review team added adverse reactions that were reported in DELTA 1 and DELTA 2 in <1% of subjects in the ANZUPGO group (application site pain, paresthesia, pruritus, erythema, and bacterial skin infections including finger cellulitis, paronychia, other skin infections, leukopenia and neutropenia.
7 DRUG INTERACTIONS	No clinically significant drug interactions have been observed or are predicted, with other prescription or over-the-counter drugs, classes of drugs, or foods. This Section has been omitted from labeling.
Contraception recommendations were not included by the review team because the safety margins observed in nonclinical studies, along with animal reproduction data, do not suggest an increased risk of adverse developmental outcomes and no appreciable risks to human fertility are expected following topical delgocitinib.
8 USE IN SPECIFIC POPULATIONS
(e.g., Pregnancy, Lactation, Females and Males of Reproductive Potential, Pediatric Use, Geriatric Use, Renal Impairment, Hepatic Impairment)	In Section 8.2 the Applicant proposed the statement: After oral administration, delgocitinib was present in the milk of lactating rats (b) (4). The review team added the language "When a drug is present in animal milk, it is likely that the drug will be present in human milk." In addition, the Applicant proposed (b) (4)
The review team removed this statement since (b) (4) (b) (4)
Refer to the nonclinical section of the review and the Maternal Health Team review in DARRTS (Reference ID: 5554590) for additional information.
9 DRUG ABUSE AND DEPENDENCE	The language under subsection 8.4 Pediatric Use and 8.5 Geriatric Use was modified to be consistent with the recommendations in the guidances for industry Pediatric Information Incorporated into Human Prescription Drug and Biological Products Labeling (March 2019) and Geriatric Information in Human Prescription Drug and Biological Product Labeling (September 2020) (when final, this guidance will represent FDA's current thinking on this topic).
10 OVERDOSAGE	ANZUPGO is not scheduled under the Controlled Substances Act and this Section has been omitted from labeling.
No specific human overdosage data are available that would be useful to the healthcare provider. This section has been omitted from labeling.
12 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY	In Section 12.2 the Applicant proposed a claim (b) (4) (b) (4)
(b) (4) The review team determined the data provided did not support this claim, therefore it was deleted (Refer to Section 19 Clinical Microbiology of this review).

However, the systemic exposure from max-use PK data were measurable, not subnanomolar, and the relationship between the level, extent, and duration of systemic exposure and the potential for adverse events is not well defined for this class. L2282: ## 24. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L2283: Clinical postmarketing requirements are intended to characterize the risks of delgocitinib use in special populations (lactating women and pediatrics) and address the safety of this product with long-term use in the target population with CHE.
L2284: ## REQUIRED PEDIATRIC ASSESSMENTS
L2285: Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) (21 U.S.C. 355c), all applications for new active ingredients (which includes new salts and new fixed combinations), new indications, new dosage forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are required to contain an assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the claimed indication(s) in pediatric patients unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or inapplicable.
L2286: We are waiving the pediatric study requirement for ages 0 to less than 11 years because the studies are impossible or highly impractical.
L2287: We are deferring submission of your pediatric study for ages 12 years to less than 18 years for this application because this product is ready for approval for use in adults and the pediatric study has not been completed.
L2288: Your deferred pediatric study required by section 505B(a) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act is a required postmarketing study. The status of this postmarketing study must be reported annually according to 21 CFR 601.28 and section 505B(a)(3)(B) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act. This required study is listed below.
L2289: ## PMR 4877-1
L2290: Conduct an assessment of the efficacy, safety, and pharmacokinetics of delgocitinib cream in adolescent subjects ages 12 to 17 years with moderate to severe chronic hand eczema.
L2291: Final Report Submission: 10/2025
L2292: ## POSTMARKETING REQUIREMENTS UNDER 505(o)
L2293: Section 505(o)(3) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act authorizes FDA to require holders of approved drug and biological product applications to conduct postmarketing studies and clinical trials for certain purposes, if FDA makes certain findings required by the statute.
L2294: NDA 219155
ANZUPGO (delgocitinib) cream, 2%
L2295: We have determined that an analysis of spontaneous postmarketing adverse events reported under subsection 505(k)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act will not be sufficient to identify the unexpected serious risks of the drug (or its active metabolites) being present in breast milk, and the effects on milk production in lactating women, and the effects on the breastfed infant.
L2296: Furthermore, the active postmarket risk identification and analysis system as available under Section 505(k)(3) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act will not be sufficient to assess this serious risk.
L2297: Therefore, based on appropriate scientific data, FDA has determined that the Applicant is required to conduct the following:
L2298: ## PMR 4877-2
L2299: Conduct a milk-only lactation study in lactating women who have received delgocitinib to measure concentrations of delgocitinib in breast milk using a validated assay. Assess the effects on the breastfed infant, if available, based on the study population.
L2300: Draft Protocol Submission:	01/2026
Final Protocol Submission:	07/2026
Study Completion:	07/2028
Final Report Submission:	01/2029
L2301: ## PMR 4877-3
L2302: Conduct a randomized, active-controlled clinical trial to evaluate the long-term safety of delgocitinib in patients with moderate to severe chronic hand eczema. The trial should be of sufficient size and duration to characterize safety events of interest (e.g., effects on bone marrow and viral reactivation).
L2303: Draft Protocol Submission:	01/2026
Final Protocol Submission:	07/2026
Trial Completion:	07/2029
Final Report Submission:	01/2030

L2403: Figure 2 is a schematic diagram illustrating the trial design for Trial DELTA 2. The trial is divided into three phases: Screening, Treatment, and Safety follow-up (or transfer to LTE trial, LP0133-1403).
Screening: 450 subjects were randomized 2:1, stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE disease severity.
Treatment: The treatment phase lasted 16 weeks (16w), from Visit 2 (Day 1) to Visit 8 (Day 113d). Subjects were assigned to either Delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g (N=300) or Cream vehicle (N=150).
Safety follow-up: Follow-up continued until Day 127d (18w).
The timeline shows visits at Week -4w (-28d*), -1w (-7d), 0w (1d), 1w (8d), 2w (15d), 4w (29d), 8w (57d), 12w (85d), 16w (113d), and 18w (127d). The Primary endpoint was assessed at Visit 8 (16w).

L2408: Figure 9 is a scatter plot titled "Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Pooled Trials DELTA 1 and DELTA 2". The X-axis represents Maximum Post-baseline ALT or AST (xULN), ranging from 0 to 10. The Y-axis represents Maximum Post-baseline Bilirubin (xULN), ranging from 0 to 3. The plot is divided into four quadrants by dashed lines at X=3 and Y=2.
Quadrant 1 (Right Upper): Potential Hy's Law (Bilirubin \ge 2 xULN, ALT/AST \ge 3 xULN).
Quadrant 2 (Left Upper): Cholestasis (Bilirubin \ge 2 xULN, ALT/AST < 3 xULN).
Quadrant 3 (Left Lower): Temple's Corollary (Bilirubin < 2 xULN, ALT/AST \ge 3 xULN).
Quadrant 4 (Right Lower): No specific label, representing cases where both ALT/AST and Bilirubin are below the thresholds.
Data points are plotted for Delgocitinib (blue dots) and Vehicle Control (white circles). A single Delgocitinib data point is circled in red, located in the Potential Hy's Law quadrant (Bilirubin \approx 2.5 xULN, ALT/AST \approx 2.5 xULN).

L2411: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
Figure 12 is a schematic timeline for Trial DELTA FORCE, detailing the study phases, participant flow, and treatment protocols.
The study began with 510 participants undergoing 1:1 randomisation during the Screening phase (Visit no. 1, Week -4, Day -28). Participants were eligible for treatment with alitretinoin and stratified by hyperkeratotic/non-hyperkeratotic subtype and region.
The Treatment phase (Weeks 0 to 16) involved two arms:
Delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g twice-daily: Discontinued at Week 16 if (based on investigator's opinion):IGA-CHE = 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) (recommended).
IGA-CHE = 4 (severe) and the participant, in the opinion of the investigator, will not benefit from further treatment.

Alitretinoin capsules 30 mg once-daily (may be reduced to 10 mg): Discontinued at Week 12 if (based on investigator's opinion):IGA-CHE = 0 (clear) or 1 (almost clear) (recommended).
IGA-CHE = 4 (severe) and the participant, in the opinion of the investigator, will not benefit from further treatment.

Participants who discontinued treatment were re-evaluated for IGA-CHE \ge 2, and if so, treatment was restarted.
The Follow-up phase (Weeks 17 to 24) included a 12-week period. At the end of the study, a pregnancy test was conducted for female participants treated with alitretinoin.
Key endpoints were assessed:
Primary endpoint (HECSI) assessed at Visit no. 12 (5 weeks after last dose).
Key secondary endpoints assessed at Visit no. 11 (2 weeks after last dose).

L2424: Figure 26: Geometric Mean (±Standard Error) Plasma Concentration Profiles of Delgocitinib. L2425: Figure 27: Plasma Concentrations Profile of Delgocitinib After Single Dose Administration Under the Fasted Condition. All doses show rapid absorption, peaking around 1-2 hours, and then declining. The 1 mg dose peaks highest (around 1000 ng/mL) and declines fastest, while the 100 mg dose peaks lower (around 500 ng/mL) and declines slower. Concentrations are near zero after 24 hours for all doses.

L2428: The first application of IMP will occur at the trial site at baseline (Day 1). The subsequent IMP applications will be performed by the subjects at home twice daily for 16 weeks. During the treatment period, the subjects will be required to return to the trial site for the visits scheduled at Weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, and 16. The last IMP application will occur at the subject's home before the subject attends the visit scheduled at Week 16.
Follow-up period
The subjects will attend a follow-up visit (performed via phone but can be a site visit if needed) approximately 2 weeks after the last IMP application for assessment of safety. Eligible subjects who complete the treatment period (i.e. who do not permanently discontinue IMP prior to Week 16) will be invited to participate in an open-label LTE trial (LP0133-1403, conducted under a separate protocol). Subjects who continue in the LTE trial will not be required to complete the follow-up period, as collection of safety data and safety surveillance will continue in the LTE trial.

* For subjects using treatments that are listed as exclusion criteria, the duration of the screening period will depend on the required wash-out period as defined in Section 8.3. In view of a 28-day wash-out for some of these treatments, the screening period can be extended up to 31 days.
Abbreviations: d = day; IGA-CHE = Investigator's Global Assessment for chronic hand eczema; LTE = long term extension; N = number of subjects; w = week.

Main assessments	
Investigator assessments of efficacy:
IGA-CHE for assessment of disease severity.
HECSI for assessment of the clinical signs of CHE.
Subject assessments of efficacy and health-related quality of life; PROs:
Hand Eczema Symptom Diary (HESD).
Hand Eczema Impact Scale (HEIS).
Dermatology Life Quality Index (DLQI).
Safety assessments:
Vital signs, physical examination, ECG, laboratory testing, subject assessment of local tolerability, and AE reporting.

Main criteria for inclusion	Age 18 years or above at screening.

Timeline diagram showing the study phases: Screening, Treatment, and Follow-up (or transfer to LTE trial, LP0133-1403). 470 subjects were randomized 2:1, stratified by region and baseline IGA-CHE disease severity. The Treatment phase compares Delgocitinib cream 20 mg/g (N=313) against Cream vehicle (N=157). The Primary endpoint is assessed at Visit 8 (Week 16, Day 113d).
L2429: HESD score
(weekly
average)	Rate the severity of any itch on your hands, at its worst, over the past 24 hours (0 no itch – 10 severe itch)
Rate the severity of any pain on your hands, at its worst, over the past 24 hours (0 no pain – 10 severe pain)
Rate the severity of any cracking of the skin on your hands, at its worst, over the past 24 hours (0 no cracking – 10 severe cracking)
Rate the severity of any redness of the skin on your hands, at its worst, over the past 24 hours (0 no redness – 10 severe redness)
Rate the severity of any dryness of the skin on your hands, at its worst, over the past 24 hours (0 no dryness – 10 severe dryness)
Rate the severity of any flaking of the skin on your hands, at its worst, over the past 24 hours (0 no flaking – 10 severe flaking)

L2431: Hand Eczema Impact Scale
Proximal Daily Activities Limitation (PDAL)	Over the past 7 days, how has your hand eczema impacted your ability to use soaps/cleaning products?
Over the past 7 days, how much has your hand eczema made it hard to do housework that involved your hands getting wet (e.g. washing dishes)?
Over the past 7 days, how much has your hand eczema made it hard to wash yourself?
Embarrassment with the appearance of the hands	Over the past 7 days, how embarrassed have you felt about how your hands look due to your hand eczema?
Over the past 7 days, how much have you disliked the appearance of your hands?
Frustration with chronic hand eczema	Over the past 7 days, how much has your hand eczema made you feel frustrated?
Sleep	Over the past 7 days, how much has your hand eczema impacted the quality of your sleep?
Work	Over the past 7 days, how much has your hand eczema made it hard to work (e.g. paid work or voluntary)?
Physical functioning	Over the past 7 days, how much has your hand eczema made it hard to hold or grip objects (e.g. a cell phone, a pen, or a pencil)?

L2433: Figure 34 is an empirical cumulative distribution function (eCDF) plot showing the cumulative percentage of subjects achieving a certain percent change in the Hand Eczema Symptom Index (HECSI) at Week 16, stratified by their change in Patient Global Assessment of Disease Severity (PaGA) from Baseline to Week 16. The plot is titled LP0133-1402.
The X-axis represents the Percent change in HECI, ranging from -100 to 100. The Y-axis represents the Cumulative percent, ranging from 0 to 100.
The legend defines 7 groups based on PaGA change:
PaGA change	Median HECI change	n
-4	-100	10
-3	-95	43
-2	-83	107
-1	-69	129
0	-35	106
1	-20	12
2	6	2
The plot shows that as the PaGA change increases (from -4 to 2), the median HECI change increases, and the cumulative distribution shifts to the right, indicating better HECI outcomes.
